Start here

Demo runbook

Every screen below was driven in a real browser overnight — 525 individual controls clicked, typed into and measured. 413 passed, 59 did not. What follows is what actually happens, not what the code suggests should happen.

Locallocalhost:8080 Present from this. No network needed. LiveCodespace Hand this to judges. Verified: 16 cameras, same data.

The six-minute route

  1. Dashboard — “this is a live station, not a mockup”
    2,000+ complaints, 9 cases, 16 feeds. Point at the 14-day chart: every bar populated, including today.
  2. VisionScan — search “red car” in Object mode
    Do: Object tab → Grouping moments ON → red car → Enter
    24 cards, each a distinct tracked vehicle with a box drawn on it. This is the moment that lands. Say: “YOLO has no class for red — the system routes around its own model’s vocabulary.”
  3. Suspect Face — upload the probe crop
    Do: Suspect Face tab → upload docs/demo-assets/suspect_face.jpg
  4. Draft FIR in Arbiter — the hand-off button on the results header
    Shows the pipeline: footage → evidence → legal instrument, with sections retrieved from a real corpus.
  5. City Map — the predictive forecast
    Where the hardest questions come. Answers are on that page, including the ablation that found two of the three terms contribute nothing.
  6. Language → हिन्दी — the whole UI switches
    Per-user setting, ~760 keys. Search queries stay English on purpose — CLIP’s text encoder is English-only. Say that before they ask.

Do not click these

  • Admin → security → “Sign out all my sessions” — logs you out instantly, mid-demo.
  • Any query phrased as a negation (“empty street with no people”) — CLIP cannot do negation and returns the busiest scene. If asked, own it: it is a property of the model, not a bug in the app.
  • Hindi or Gujarati PDF documents — the UI translates, but ReportLab does no Indic shaping so the PDF body renders malformed. Demo PDFs in English.
  • Do not run demo_reset.py before presenting — it wipes the closed cases, evidence and ratings that make the dashboard look alive.

Logins

Station Adminadmin@city.gov admin123Everything. Use this.
Officerofficer@city.gov officer123For showing role-based access.

If something breaks

  • A screen is empty or spinning — reload the tab first. Most one-off failures overnight were the backend restarting, not a real fault.
  • Thumbnails show as broken images — you hit the rate limiter. Wait ten seconds and re-run the search.
  • The live link is down — switch to local and keep going. Say the hosted copy is a convenience; the product is offline-first by design. That is a genuine strength, not a save.
  • Do not debug on stage. Move to the next screen and come back.
Login / auth →
1 of 27

Login / auth — the front door

The first screen judges see: a branded sign-in with visible demo credentials, four sub-flows (sign in, register, forgot, reset), six working legal/help modals and eight tap-to-call helplines — with one reachable input that white-screens the entire application.

Get there: http://localhost:8080 — the unauthenticated landing screen

Show this

  1. Wrong password and unknown email return the exact same message: 'Invalid email or password'. No timing tell, no 'user not found'.
    Do: Type admin@city.gov with a wrong password → 'Invalid email or password'. Then nobody@nowhere.com with any password → the identical message.
    Deliberate anti-enumeration. A judge probing for a security answer gets a textbook one on the first screen — you cannot use the login form to discover which officers have accounts.
  2. The screen ships its own demo credentials in a labelled panel — all four roles, email and password, in monospace.
    Do: Point at the 'Demo accounts' panel below the sign-in card: admin@city.gov/admin123, lead@city.gov/lead123, officer@city.gov/officer123, citizen@example.com/citizen123.
    Judges can drive the platform themselves without asking. It also quietly communicates the RBAC story before anyone logs in: Station Admin, Team Lead, Officer, Citizen are four genuinely different products behind one door.
  3. 'Forgot password?' returns the same reply for a known and an unknown email — 'If the email exists, a reset token was sent' — and tells you how to complete it offline: check the admin outbox, then use 'Have a token?'.
    Do: Forgot password? → enter citizen@example.com → read the message → back → 'Have a token?' → a bogus token gives a clean 'Invalid or expired token'.
    Anti-enumeration applied consistently, plus a password reset that works with no mail server. The offline-first thinking runs through the whole product and shows up here on the front door.
  4. Below the fold: eight tap-to-call helplines — 1930 for cyber/financial fraud, 112, 100, 1091, 1098, 14567, 108, 101 — plus six working modals for About, Contact, Help/FAQ, Privacy Policy, Terms & Disclaimer and Accessibility.
    Do: Scroll to the footer → tap a helpline number → click each of the six footer links to open its modal.
    A citizen who lands here in a crisis and cannot remember their password can still reach help in one tap. And having Accessibility and Privacy as real content, not dead links, is what separates a hackathon build from something a government body could actually host.
  5. 'New citizen? Register' creates a real account and signs you straight in, landing on the citizen's 'My Complaints' with the correct three-item nav.
    Do: Register → name, email, password of at least 6 characters, optional phone → Create account. WARNING: use 6+ characters, see broken.
    The public can self-serve onto the platform in one form — no officer provisioning step — and role assignment is automatic and correct.

If they ask

Why do a wrong password and an unknown email give the same error?
Deliberate anti-enumeration. If the messages differed, the login form becomes an oracle for discovering which email addresses have accounts — useful for targeting officers with phishing. The same rule is applied to the forgot-password flow, which returns 'If the email exists, a reset token was sent' regardless. Both responses come back on the same path in comparable time, around 2.5 seconds.
You're showing real passwords on the login screen. Isn't that a problem?
Yes, and it's gated for exactly that reason — the panel is dev-only demo seeding, and the seed module is explicit that these are DEV ONLY credentials controlled by a settings.seed_demo_users flag. It exists so judges can drive four roles without waiting on us. For any real deployment the flag is off, the accounts don't exist, and the panel doesn't render. I'd want to see that verified in the deployment checklist rather than take it on trust.
How are sessions handled — JWT, cookies, refresh tokens?
A bearer JWT in localStorage, injected by an axios request interceptor. The payload carries sub, role, an exp and a tv field — token_version — which is the revocation mechanism: bump the user's token_version and every outstanding token for them stops validating. There is no refresh token; when the JWT expires you sign in again. A response interceptor drops the token and reloads on a 401, deliberately excluding the login call itself so a wrong password doesn't cause a reload loop.
What stops someone brute-forcing these logins?
A token-bucket rate limiter keyed on (IP, route class): 10 requests per minute for auth routes, 120 for the general API, 20 for uploads, with a Retry-After header on the 429. Worth flagging for tomorrow specifically — it's per IP, so if several judges try the demo accounts from the same conference wifi behind one NAT, they share the 10/min budget and will start getting locked out. If more than two people will be logging in at once, that number wants raising before the session.
What is the single worst bug on this screen?
Registering with a password shorter than six characters white-screens the entire application. The server correctly returns 422, but FastAPI's 422 body puts detail as an ARRAY of validation objects, and the client does setError(err?.response?.data?.detail || ...) and renders it — React refuses to render an object as a child and throws error #31, which unmounts everything to the ErrorBoundary. Only a Reload button remains. The same pattern exists at 15 call sites, and I confirmed a second demo-reachable one: Admin → Add staff user with a short password crashes identically. The root-cause fix is two lines in the axios response interceptor that already exists in frontend/src/api.js — normalise an array-shaped detail into a string once, and all 15 sites are fixed. I did not apply it because other agents are testing this stack and it needs a frontend rebuild.
Can I reset a password with no mail server?
Yes, that's what the 'Offline demo' wording is about. Forgot password generates a token; instead of relying on SMTP it tells you to read it from the admin outbox or the server log, then paste it into the 'Have a token?' form. The whole platform is built to run air-gapped — same reasoning as Arbiter's offline mode and the bundled legal corpus — so the auth flows had to work without outbound mail too.
Is the login screen translated?
Yes, and it's done cleverly: it reads the last language this browser used out of localStorage, so if an officer was working in Hindi and signs out, the sign-in page is already Hindi before anyone authenticates. The gap is that there's no language switcher ON the login screen — if the laptop was last used in Gujarati, a judge can't change it until after signing in.
What happens if the backend is slow or momentarily unreachable?
Worse than it should. The session bootstrap at auth.jsx:16-18 is API.authMe().then(setUser).catch(() => API.setToken(null)) — the catch is unconditional, so ANY failure, including a 429 or a transient network blip, throws away a perfectly valid token and drops the user back to the login screen. It should only clear on a 401. I hit this repeatedly while the API was rate-limited during testing: reload the page at the wrong moment and you're logged out. One-line fix, but it's a real risk if the demo box gets busy.
Are there security headers, or is this a bare dev server?
They're there — x-content-type-options, x-frame-options, referrer-policy, permissions-policy and a content-security-policy on API responses, applied by a middleware that sits outermost specifically so the headers are stamped even on 429 and 423 short-circuits. Passwords are hashed, never stored plaintext.

Careful / known limits

  • CRITICAL — DO NOT DEMO: registering with a password shorter than 6 characters crashes the ENTIRE app to a white 'Something went wrong / Minified React error #31' screen with only a 'Reload' button. Reproduced 100% of the time. Root cause: FastAPI returns 422 with detail as an ARRAY of objects; frontend/src/components/platform/LoginView.jsx:36 does setError(err?.response?.data?.detail || …) and renders the array, which React refuses. FIX (2 lines, in the interceptor that already exists at frontend/src/api.js:21-31): if Array.isArray(err?.response?.data?.detail) replace it with detail.map(e => e.msg).join('; '). That one change fixes all 15 call sites.
  • SAME CRASH, second demo-reachable path: Admin → Add staff user with a password under 6 characters crashes identically (confirmed, HTTP 422 on /api/users). If you demo user management, always type a 6+ character temp password.
  • 13 more call sites share the crash pattern and are one bad 422 away from the same white screen: App.jsx:173, ArbiterPanel.jsx:66, GovIntelPanel.jsx:115/138/204/222, AdminView.jsx:122/471/654, AnalyticsView.jsx:47, CaseWorkspace.jsx:190/221, SceneAnalytics.jsx:81/117.
  • frontend/src/auth.jsx:16-18 — API.authMe().then(setUser).catch(() => API.setToken(null)) discards the auth token on ANY error, not just a 401. A 429 or a momentary network blip on page reload silently logs the user out. I hit this repeatedly under load. Fix: only clear the token when e?.response?.status === 401.
  • Login rate limit is 10 requests/minute per IP. Several judges signing in from the same NAT'd conference wifi will share that budget and start seeing 429s. Consider raising rate_limit_login_per_min in backend/app/config.py:159 before the session if more than two people will log in concurrently.
  • No language switcher on the login screen. It inherits the browser's last-used language from localStorage with no way to change it until after sign-in — so if the laptop was last driven in Gujarati, the sign-in page opens in Gujarati.
  • The ErrorBoundary crash screen is English-only ('Something went wrong', 'Reload') — it is a class component and cannot use the i18n hook. So a Hindi demo that hits any crash shows an English wall.
  • Demo credentials for all four accounts are printed in plaintext on the login screen. It is flag-gated dev seeding and correct for the demo, but confirm the flag is off in any deployed build before this is shown to anyone outside the room.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 4 seeded demo accounts across 4 roles: admin, lead, officer, citizen
  • 4 login sub-flows: sign in, register, forgot password, reset with token
  • Rate limits: 10 auth requests/min, 120 general API requests/min, 20 uploads/min — token bucket per (IP, route class) with Retry-After
  • Wrong-password round trip: ~2.5s, HTTP 401
  • 6 footer modals, all functional: About, Contact, Help / FAQ, Privacy Policy, Terms & Disclaimer, Accessibility
  • 8 tap-to-call helplines: 1930, 112, 100, 1091, 1098, 14567, 108, 101
  • Minimum password length: 6 characters (enforced server-side; under 6 crashes the client)
  • Page load to interactive: ~3s, 0 page errors
  • JWT claims: sub, role, tv (token_version, for revocation), exp
  • 5 security headers on API responses, stamped even on 429/423 short-circuits

2 of 27

Sign-in screen — auth flows + institutional footer

The screen every judge sees first, and the only screen where the platform states its institutional posture — helplines, privacy, GIGW/WCAG accessibility, IT Act terms — none of which appears in the coverage document I was given.

Get there: http://localhost:8080 signed out. Four states reachable from the sign-in card: Sign in (default), "New citizen? Register", "Forgot password?", "Have a token?". Six footer buttons open modals: About / Contact / Help & FAQ / Privacy Policy / Terms & Disclaimer / Accessibility.

Show this

  1. Eight live Indian helpline numbers are printed on the sign-in page before anyone logs in — 1930 cyber fraud, 112, 100, 1091 women, 1098 child, 14567 elderline, 108, 101.
    Do: Do not skip past the login screen. Let it sit for three seconds and point at the helpline strip while typing.
    A citizen who cannot log in still gets the number they actually need. It is a two-second point that reads as "built by someone who has been in a police station", and it sits on screen for the whole time the presenter is typing the password.
  2. Footer → Accessibility opens a real conformance statement: WCAG 2.1 AA and the Guidelines for Indian Government Websites (GIGW), keyboard navigation, colour-never-the-only-signal, prefers-reduced-motion.
    Do: Footer → "Accessibility". Then "Privacy Policy" (role-based need-to-know, data stays in the department) and "Terms & Disclaimer" (AI outputs are investigative aids, misuse is an offence under IT Act 2000).
    GIGW is the compliance gate for any actual Indian government deployment. Almost no hackathon project has heard of it. If a judge is from the buyer side rather than the tech side, this is the single most credible thing on the login page.
  3. Full self-service auth: citizen self-registration, forgot-password with a token, and reset-by-token — and the forgot-password flow tells the honest truth about being offline.
    Do: "Forgot password?" → type admin@city.gov → "Send reset link" → read the amber line aloud.
    Requesting a reset returns "If the email exists, a reset token was sent. Offline demo: check the admin outbox / server log, then use 'Have a token?'". It does not leak whether the account exists (correct security behaviour) and it does not pretend an SMTP server exists.

Exact inputs

FieldValue — click to copyWhat you should get

If they ask

You print the demo passwords on the login page. Is that in the product?
It is a demo affordance controlled by settings.seed_demo_users — the four seeded accounts and their passwords are rendered so a judge can sign in as any role instantly without asking. In a deployment the seeding flag is off and the block does not render. It is worth pre-empting rather than letting a security-minded judge point at it first.
Does the forgot-password flow leak which emails are registered?
No. It returns the same message whether or not the account exists — "If the email exists, a reset token was sent" — which is the standard non-enumerating response. What it does not have is an SMTP sender, so offline the token has to be read from the admin outbox or the server log, and the UI says exactly that instead of pretending mail was sent.
Is the accessibility statement real or aspirational?
Aspirational in the honest sense: it states the target (WCAG 2.1 AA and GIGW) and the specific measures taken — keyboard navigation with visible focus, colour never the only carrier of meaning, reduce-motion respected. It has not been through a formal audit, and I would say that rather than claim certification.

Careful / known limits

  • The sign-in card renders all four demo account passwords in plain text. Correct for a hackathon, and the presenter should name it as a demo flag before a judge does — otherwise it reads as a security lapse on the very first screen.
  • "Have a token?" / Reset password could not be completed end to end — no reset token is surfaced anywhere in the UI, only in the admin outbox or the server log. Do not offer to demonstrate a full password reset.
  • The six footer modals are the only place the institutional posture is stated, and nothing in the app links to them once you are signed in except the same footer. Easy to forget they exist.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 8 helpline numbers rendered on the signed-out page: 1930, 112, 100, 1091, 1098, 14567, 108, 101
  • 4 auth states (sign in / register / forgot / reset-by-token) and 6 footer modals — 10 surfaces, all render, 0 JS errors
  • Register form: 4 fields, 3 required
  • 0 uncaught page errors and 0 API responses ≥400 across every auth state and modal

3 of 27

Command Dashboard (Analytics)

One auto-refreshing command view that reconciles 2,001 citizen complaints, the whole case pipeline, citizen satisfaction ratings and the LIVE VisionScan vector index into ten KPI tiles and six charts, with no charting library and no network dependency.

Get there: Log in at http://localhost:8080 as admin@city.gov / admin123 — staff land on the Dashboard automatically. Otherwise click "Dashboard" (first item) in the top nav.

Show this

  1. Ten KPI tiles that reconcile two different systems on one row — the CityShield case database AND the running VisionScan FAISS index. "INDEXED FRAMES 417" is read from the live index object (get_clip_index().ntotal), not from a table.
    Do: Log in as admin@city.gov / admin123. The dashboard is the landing page. Read across the top row, then drop to the second row and point at FEEDS 16 / INDEXED FRAMES 417 and say "those two come from the live vector index, not the database."
    A judge expects a dashboard to be SELECT COUNT(*) over one DB. This one proves the AI search index and the policing case system are the same product: 16 camera feeds, 417 CLIP-embedded frames, 2,001 complaints, 10 cases, all in one header. I verified every tile against /api/analytics/summary and they matched exactly, digit for digit.
  2. The 14-day complaints trend — 14 bars, every single one populated, hover gives the exact date and count.
    Do: Hover the rightmost bar — the native tooltip reads "2026-08-19: 7". Hover the tallest (leftmost or 08-16) — "2026-08-06: 16".
    Dense, gap-filled series with no holes: I read the title attribute off all 14 bars and none was zero (16,14,14,16,11,10,14,9,8,11,16,8,12,7 = 166 complaints in 14 days). The backend fills missing days with 0 rather than dropping them, so the axis never lies about a quiet day.
  3. Closed-loop service metrics: AVG RESOLVE 115h and AVG RATING 4.2/5 (5) sitting next to OPEN CASES / CLOSED.
    Do: Point at the AVG RESOLVE and AVG RATING tiles together and say "complaint in, case out, citizen rates it, and that rating is on this screen."
    Most hackathon dashboards show inputs (complaints filed). This shows OUTCOMES — how long a case actually took to close, computed as AVG((julianday(closed_at)-julianday(created_at))*24) over closed cases, and what the citizen scored it afterwards. It is the citizen-portal loop closing in a number.
  4. Six breakdown widgets — cases-by-status donut, complaints-by-category, complaints-by-status, cases-by-severity, top complaint locations — all hand-rolled SVG/CSS with zero charting dependency.
    Do: If asked about the stack: "frontend/src/components/platform/charts.jsx — 104 lines, three primitives, no chart library, so it works with the network cable pulled."
    No Chart.js, no Recharts, no CDN. The whole dashboard renders offline in an air-gapped police network, which is exactly the deployment constraint a government buyer cares about. The donut is a single <svg> with stroke-dasharray arcs.
  5. It is genuinely live — measured, not claimed.
    Do: Say it, don't wait for it on stage. (If you want to demo it: have a second person close a case, then watch CLOSED tick up within 15s.)
    The header says "live · refreshes every 15s". I counted the outbound /api/analytics/summary calls: exactly one extra in a 17-second window. If someone converts a complaint to a case in another tab, this screen catches it within 15s.

If they ask

Is "417 indexed frames" a database row count or the actual search index?
The actual index. analytics.py imports get_clip_index() and reads .ntotal off the running FAISS object — it is the only KPI on this page that does not come from SQLite. It happens to equal COUNT(*) FROM frames (417) right now, which is the correct invariant: every extracted keyframe got embedded.
2,001 complaints — is that real Ahmedabad crime data?
No, and we say so on the City Map footer and in docs/AHMEDABAD_CRIME_DATA.md. It is a deterministic synthetic 180-day stream generated from public NCRB/press-derived area profiles (seed_synthetic.py, fixed RNG seed). The 9 cases, 30 evidence items and 5 ratings on this screen are real actions taken through the real UI.
How is "avg resolve 115h" computed?
AVG((julianday(closed_at) - julianday(created_at)) * 24.0) over cases WHERE status='closed' AND closed_at IS NOT NULL. Cases still open contribute nothing, so it is a survivor-biased number by construction — it answers "how long did the ones we finished take", not "how long does a case take".
Open 4 + closed 6 = 10 cases, but 2,001 complaints. Where did the other 1,991 go?
Complaints and cases are different entities. A complaint is a citizen report; it becomes a case only when an officer triages and converts it. The complaints-by-status chart shows exactly 9 in state 'converted' — those are the ones that became cases. The other 1,992 sit in submitted (809) / under_review (799) / assigned (384). That ratio is deliberate: it is what a real station backlog looks like.
What charting library is this?
None. charts.jsx is 104 lines of hand-written SVG and CSS — three primitives (BarList, Donut, TrendBars). No Chart.js, no Recharts, no CDN font or script. The whole page renders with the network cable pulled, which matters for a police intranet deployment.
How live is "live"?
15-second poll of /api/analytics/summary — I measured exactly one extra request in a 17-second window. It is polling, not websockets; that is a deliberate choice because the numbers are aggregates that change on the order of minutes, and polling survives a proxy that kills long-lived connections.
Why does the users tile say 5 when you seed 4 accounts?
Because someone registered a citizen account through the public register form during testing — the tile is genuinely live. The four seeded accounts are admin@city.gov, lead@city.gov, officer@city.gov and citizen@example.com.
Can I filter this by date range or station?
Not on this screen — honestly, no. The window is a fixed 14 days and /api/analytics/summary takes no parameters. The date/category filtering lives on the City Map (Reports layer), which has category and 7/30/90/all-time selectors.
What happens if the backend is slow or down?
The page shows a spinner until the first response, then an error box on failure, and retries every 15s. The failure mode I actually hit was a 429 from the per-IP rate limiter, which blanks the whole dashboard to "Too many requests" until the next poll. That is a real weakness — the error branch is all-or-nothing rather than keeping the last good render.

Careful / known limits

  • TOMORROW MORNING THE LAST BAR WILL BE ZERO. Verified, not guessed: I replayed the backend's exact dense-fill query against the live DB with 'now' set to 2026-08-20 04:00 UTC (~09:30 IST) and got Aug 07..Aug 19 with real values and 2026-08-20 = 0. Cause: the chart window uses SQLite date('now'), which is UTC and rolls over at 05:30 IST, while the synthetic complaint stream is a ONE-SHOT marker-gated insert whose newest row is 2026-08-19 16:46:52 UTC. The seed does not roll forward. So the fix you made today is correct for today only. Options before the demo: (a) accept it — it is one 1px stub at the right edge of 14 bars and nobody will notice; (b) shift the newest complaints forward by one day; (c) re-seed. Do NOT run seed with force — it INSERTs again without clearing and would double the stream to ~4,000 complaints.
  • KPI DRIFT — the numbers in your brief are already stale. Measured at 00:52: closed 5, avg resolve 138h, evidence 28, total cases 9, users 4. Measured at 01:20 the same night: closed 6, avg resolve 115h, evidence 30, total cases 10, users 5. Complaints (2001), feeds (16) and indexed frames (417) did NOT move. This is concurrent testing writing to the demo DB, not a bug — but re-read the tiles on the morning and do not read numbers off a script.
  • A SINGLE 429 BLANKS THE ENTIRE DASHBOARD. AnalyticsView's error branch replaces every tile and chart with one red "Too many requests" box. I hit this repeatedly. Root cause worth knowing: the limiter is 120 req/min keyed on request.client.host, and because nginx proxies /api the backend sees the nginx container IP for EVERY browser — so all browsers share ONE bucket. One idle tab already burns 70 req/min (measured). Two tabs open on this stack ≈ 140/min and everything starts 429-ing. On stage: keep exactly one tab open, and close any other machine pointed at the app.
  • No manual refresh button and no date-range control. If a judge asks to see last quarter, the answer is "not on this screen" — pivot to the City Map's 7/30/90/all-time selector.
  • Cases-by-severity renders as a single "High 9" bar. It is correct (all seeded cases are high severity) but it looks like a broken chart on a projector. Don't linger on it.
  • Cosmetic: the AVG RATING tile renders "4.2/5 (5)" — the trailing (5) is the rating count and reads like a typo at a glance.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 16 camera feeds, CAM-01..CAM-16, all status 'ready'
  • 417 indexed frames — read from the live FAISS index ntotal, equals COUNT(*) FROM frames

VisionScan / Text search

Natural-language search over 417 indexed keyframes from 16 cameras: CLIP embeds the whole frame, so you describe a scene in plain English and get the moments back, collapsed into events.

Get there: Log in as admin@city.gov / admin123 → top nav "VisionScan" → "Text" tab (default). Search box placeholder: e.g. "person in red jacket near the gate".

Show this

  1. One English sentence searches 16 cameras at once and returns distinct moments, not a wall of near-identical frames.
    Do: Type: a person carrying a backpack → Enter. Point at the "18" badge and the 00:00:00–00:00:24 span on card 1.
    "a person carrying a backpack" returns 8 cards in 80 ms. Each card is a whole EVENT — the top one carries a badge reading 18 frames spanning 00:00:00–00:00:24 on CAM-10. Grouping OFF returns 40 raw frames of the same footage. The judge sees the system doing the investigator's triage for them.
  2. The app notices when text search is the wrong tool and offers the right one, before you get a bad result.
    Do: Type: red car (do not press Enter). The nudge banner appears immediately.
    Type a query containing an object noun and a banner appears unprompted: "Whole-frame text match is fuzzy — Find every object scores each detected object for precise matches", with a one-click button. This is the product anticipating the failure mode rather than hiding it.
  3. The same query, two engines, side by side: 1 fuzzy scene vs 24 individually-boxed objects.
    Do: Search: red car (1 match) → click "Find every object: OFF" → search again (24 matches, boxes drawn).
    Plain text "red car" returns 1 match. Flip "Find every object: OFF" → ON, same query, and you get 24 hits, each a specific tracked object with a green box drawn on it. It is the clearest possible demonstration of why per-object retrieval exists.
  4. It honestly returns nothing when there is nothing.
    Do: Search: underwater coral reef scuba diver
    "underwater coral reef scuba diver" returns "0 matches" and a clean empty state, not a page of confident garbage. A relevance floor (text_min_score 0.22 plus a relative floor at 0.88× the best hit) is doing real work.

If they ask

VisionScan has Text search and Object search — what is the difference?
Text embeds the WHOLE FRAME with CLIP and finds scenes: one 512-d vector per keyframe, cosine-matched against the embedded sentence. It answers "where does this situation happen". Object runs a YOLO label filter over the detections table first, and when the phrase isn't one of YOLO's classes it falls back to per-detection CLIP on the cropped object regions — that's what lets "red car" find a specific car and draw a box on it. Concretely on this data: plain text "red car" returns 1 fuzzy scene; per-object returns 24 individually-boxed cars.
What does "Grouping moments" actually do?
Two different things depending on the search, and that is deliberate. For text, YOLO-label and face search it clusters frames from the same camera that are within event_gap_sec (6 s) of each other and keeps the highest-scoring one as the representative — so 40 raw frames of one person loitering become 8 distinct moments. For per-object (region) search it groups on track_id instead, because two different red cars visible in the same second are two results, not one; time-clustering would wrongly merge them.
Those scores are only 25–33%. Is that a bad match?
Those are raw CLIP cosine similarities, and for text-to-image CLIP the usable range is compressed — roughly 0.18 is noise and 0.33 is a strong hit on this data. What matters is the ranking and the relative floor: we cut anything below 0.22 absolute and below 0.88× the top hit. That is why "underwater coral reef scuba diver" returns zero instead of a page of 0.20s. We do not present the raw number as a confidence probability.
Can it handle negation — "a street with NO people"?
No, and this is a real limitation of CLIP, not a bug in our code. CLIP has no logical operators; it embeds the bag of concepts, so "no people" pulls toward frames about people and streets. Measured: "empty street with no people" returns 5 hits and all 5 contain detected people. Worse, per-object "no people" returns 60 hits topped by stop signs and a fire hydrant. The honest workaround is structural, not semantic: use Object search for "person" with grouping on and read the frames where the count is zero — the detections table can answer absence, the embedding cannot.
How large is the index, and would this scale to a real city?
Right now: 417 keyframes, 3,362 detections, 956 faces, 1,409 tracks across 16 cameras, in FAISS with SQLite for metadata. Warm text search is 80 ms median. FAISS flat search is linear, so at city scale you would move to an IVF or HNSW index and shard by camera or time window — the retrieval layer is already abstracted behind get_clip_index(). The honest gap is ingestion, not search: we index at roughly 2 keyframes/second of footage on CPU, so real-time coverage of hundreds of cameras needs GPU workers, not a different algorithm.
Why keyframes and not every frame?
Keyframe sampling at ~2 fps. Every frame would multiply the index ~15× for almost no recall gain, since consecutive frames are near-identical — that is the same redundancy the grouping toggle exists to hide. The cost is that fast-moving objects can be missed between keyframes, which is exactly what the Scene Analytics quality badge reports per camera.
Is any of this fabricated or pre-seeded for the demo?
The footage is real stock CCTV-style video ingested through the same pipeline a station would use; the searches run live against FAISS at request time. Nothing on this screen is pre-computed for the demo — you can type an arbitrary query and watch it return in under 100 ms. What is not real is the case metadata elsewhere in the platform, which is seeded.

Careful / known limits

  • DEMO-CRITICAL — thumbnails are rate-limited and result grids render as black boxes. /thumbnails/ falls into the same 120 req/min default bucket as the API. Measured: 150 sequential thumbnail requests → first HTTP 429 at request #114. In the browser, a 10-card grid had 9 of 10 thumbnails 429'd and a 60-card grid had 53 of 60 broken — bounding boxes floating on empty black cards. Root cause chain: (1) RateLimitMiddleware._classify in backend/app/security_mw.py sends anything that isn't /auth/login, /api/videos POST, /api/search/image|face or /api/admin/export/ to the 'default' 120/min bucket, which includes /thumbnails/; (2) it keys on request.client.host, which behind the nginx container is 172.20.0.3 for EVERY browser (confirmed in backend logs), even though nginx does set X-Real-IP; (3) one idle VisionScan tab already makes 66 API calls/min (measured over 60 s: health 20, videos 20, anomalies/summary 12, notifications 12, broadcasts 2). So a single presenter idling burns 55% of the budget, and one 60-card search finishes it off. Suggested one-line fix in _classify: return early with no limit when request.url.path.startswith('/thumbnails/'). Requires a backend restart, so I did not apply it. If you cannot restart: keep grouping ON (24 cards, not 60), avoid re-running searches rapidly, and close every other browser tab pointed at the app.
  • CLIP cannot do negation. "empty street with no people" returns 5 hits and every single one contains detected people (2, 1, 3, 1, 1). The often-repeated phrasing that it returns "the busiest scene" is an overstatement — the top hit has 2 people, while the crowded-market top hit has 7 — so do not say that on stage. The defensible line is: "CLIP has no NOT operator; every frame it returned still contains people." The sharper illustration if a judge pushes: per-object search for "no people" returns 60 hits led by stop signs and a fire hydrant.
  • "Find every object" hides the "Grouping moments" toggle and the state leaks across tabs. In frontend/src/components/SearchPanel.jsx the grouping toggle is rendered under {!perObject && (…)}, and switchMode() resets mode/file/group but never resets perObject. Consequence: enable "Find every object" on the Text tab, switch to the Object tab, and that tab now has ZERO toggles — the presenter cannot turn grouping on or off any more, with no indication why. Recovery is to switch back to Text and toggle per-object off. Avoid touching that toggle if you plan to move between tabs.
  • The report request has no user-facing error path. When POST /api/report was rate-limited, the UI threw an uncaught 'Request failed with status code 429' as a page error and the modal simply sat there — no toast, no message, no retry. On stage a throttled report looks like a hung button.
  • Camera scoping can return MORE hits than searching all 16 cameras. Per-object "red car" unscoped returns 24; scoped to CAM-12 it returns 38. That is because the relative relevance floor is 0.88 × the best score IN THE FILTERED SET — remove CAM-14's strong 0.3055 hit and the floor drops, letting weaker CAM-12 cars through. Defensible if explained, but do not show the two counts side by side without being ready for the question.
  • The camera rail is ordered by created_at DESC, so it reads CAM-16, 14, 15, 13, 12, 10, 11, 09, 08, 07, 04, 05, 06, 03, 01, 02 — scrambled to anyone expecting numeric order. Cosmetic only.
  • DO NOT click "Re-index" in the camera-feeds rail header. It POSTs /api/reindex, which queues a full pipeline re-run over all 16 ready videos as background tasks on a CPU-only backend. I did not click it and neither should the presenter.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 16 cameras, 417 indexed keyframes, 956 indexed faces — all three confirmed against the live DB
  • 3,362 object detections, 3,355 of them with per-crop CLIP embeddings, 1,409 distinct tracks
  • Warm text search: 80 ms median server-side (5 runs, 50–80 ms range)
  • "a person carrying a backpack": 8 grouped events vs 40 raw frames; top hit CAM-10, 28%, 18 frames over 00:00:00–00:00:24
  • "crowded market street": 11 grouped events; top CAM-09, 28%, 13 frames over 00:00:00–00:00:20
  • "red car" as plain text: 1 match. Same words with per-object ON: 24 matches. A 24× difference from one toggle.
  • Relevance floors in use: text_min_score 0.22 absolute plus a relative floor at 0.88 × the top score
  • Idle cost of one open VisionScan tab: 66 API requests per minute against a 120/min shared limit

5 of 27

VisionScan / Object search

Two engines behind one box: an exact YOLO-label filter over 3,362 stored detections, and — when the phrase isn't a YOLO class — an automatic fallback to per-detection CLIP on object crops, which is what makes "red car" return specific boxed cars.

Get there: VisionScan → "Object" tab. Placeholder: e.g. car, person, truck, motorcycle. Eight label chips sit under the search box. Note this tab opens with Grouping moments OFF by design.

Show this

  1. Type "red car", which is not a YOLO class, and it silently falls back to per-object CLIP and draws a box on each matching car across 7 cameras.
    Do: Object tab → click "Grouping moments: OFF" so it reads ON → type: red car → Enter. 24 cards with boxes.
    The header openly says "not an exact object label — showing per-object visual matches (CLIP) instead". 24 grouped hits, top one a tracked car on CAM-14 with 32 frames over 00:00:00–00:00:16, green box on the car. YOLO has no concept of "red"; the system routes around its own model's vocabulary limit and tells you it did.
  2. The grouping toggle turns 40 near-duplicate frames into 17 real objects, and shows its work.
    Do: Click chip "car" (40 cards) → click "Grouping moments: OFF" → click chip "car" again (17 cards, badge 56).
    Chip "car" with grouping OFF: 40 cards, all near-identical. Grouping ON: 17 cards, and the top one carries a badge reading 56 frames spanning 00:00:00–00:00:28 on CAM-12 — one bus-route camera, one car, 28 seconds. The badge is the audit trail for the collapse.
  3. Eight one-click YOLO chips, every one of which returns real hits at 30 ms.
    Do: Click any chip. "car" and "bus" give the highest confidences (95%).
    person, car, truck, motorcycle, bicycle, bus, backpack, handbag — all eight work, with top confidences of 93%, 95%, 90%, 91%, 88%, 95%, 82%, 60%. A judge can pick any chip at random and it lands. 30 ms median because it is a plain indexed SQL query, not a model run.
  4. Each hit opens to show the underlying detections with per-object confidences.
    Do: Search red car → click card 1.
    Clicking a per-object card shows the source file, the frame number, and the ranked detections in that frame — e.g. traffic_redcar_06.mp4, frame #2236, car 60% / person 54% / potted plant 49%. It is traceable back to evidence, not a black box.

If they ask

What happens when I type something YOLO has never heard of?
The UI tries the exact label filter first. If that returns nothing, it automatically re-issues the query against the per-detection CLIP index — every detected object crop has its own embedding, 3,355 of them — and labels the result banner "not an exact object label — showing per-object visual matches (CLIP) instead". So "red car" works even though YOLO's 80 classes contain "car" but no colours.
Why does grouping give 24 but ungrouped gives exactly 60? That looks like a round number.
Good catch — 60 is a cap, not a total. The UI requests top_k=60 for per-object search. If you raise it, 78 crops pass the relevance filter and collapse to the same 24 tracked objects. So the honest statement is: 78 individual detections match, showing the first 60, which are 24 distinct physical objects.
How do you know two detections are the same car rather than two cars?
We do not guess from time or position — we use the track_id the tracker already assigned during ingestion, grouping on (video_id, label, track_id). That matters: two different red cars visible in the same second are two results, not one, which naive time-clustering would wrongly merge. Detections with no track_id are deliberately left ungrouped rather than lumped together, since we cannot know.
That top hit is labelled "truck" but it is clearly a car. Is your detector wrong?
Yes, on that instance. YOLO mislabels some vehicles in this footage, and we surface the label rather than hiding it. It matters less than it looks, because the per-object CLIP fallback ranks on the visual embedding of the crop, not on the label — that hit surfaced because the crop looks like a red car, regardless of what YOLO called it. The label chip is shown so the operator can see and discount the disagreement.
Can this ever return nothing when nothing matches?
The YOLO-label path can — an unused label returns zero. The per-object CLIP path effectively cannot, and that is a real weakness: the absolute floor is 0.22 but object crops sit at a higher noise floor, so "elephant in a swimming pool" still returns 13 cars and people. The whole-frame text path does return zero correctly. If a judge wants to see an honest empty result, use Text search, not this tab.
Is the bounding box computed at search time or stored?
Stored at ingestion, in the video's native pixel space, then converted to fractions of the frame at query time so the box lands correctly on a thumbnail scaled to a 480 px long edge. Rows from before that migration emit no box at all rather than a wrong one — a wrong box on evidence is worse than no box.
Why is object search 30 ms but text search 80 ms?
Object-label search never touches a model — it is an indexed SQL aggregate over the detections table. Text search has to embed the query string through CLIP's text encoder first. Per-object CLIP lands in between at 70 ms because it embeds the text once then does a FAISS lookup over 3,355 crop vectors.

Careful / known limits

  • The frame-count badge lies about what the detail view will show, on the flagship demo. Per-object hits grouped by track_id display a badge like "32" and a span 00:00:00–00:00:16, but opening that card shows ONE image, no gallery strip and no "Add all to report" button. Root cause: _group_by_track() in backend/app/core/query_router.py sets event_count / event_start_hms / event_end_hms but never populates event_frames, whereas the time-based _event_from_cluster() does. Verified by contrast in the same session: YOLO chip "car" badge 56 → modal shows "56 instances" and 57 images; face search badge 13 → modal shows 13 images; per-object "red car" badge 32 → modal shows 1 image. This bites exactly the "red car" story you most want to tell. Mitigation for tomorrow: demo the gallery from a YOLO chip (car / person) or from the face search, NOT from a per-object result. If you want the 32 frames of that car, turn grouping OFF and select the cards directly.
  • Duplicate result cards when grouping is OFF on per-object search. "red car" ungrouped renders 60 cards from only 51 distinct frames, because two detections in the same frame (different track_ids) each become their own hit while App.jsx keys the grid on hit.frame_id and stores selection in a Set of frame_id. Observed consequence: clicking the select control on one card visually marks TWO cards as selected while the tray reads "1 frames selected". React will also be logging duplicate-key warnings. Avoid selecting frames for a report while grouping is OFF on this tab.
  • "Find every object" state leaks from the Text tab and can strip this tab of all its toggles. If per-object was enabled on the Text tab, switching here leaves the Object tab with zero visible toggles — no grouping control at all. Fix is to go back to Text and turn it off. Same root cause as reported on the Text page (SearchPanel.jsx: switchMode never resets perObject; the grouping toggle renders under {!perObject && …}).
  • The per-object path cannot return an empty result. region_min_score is 0.22, which is below the noise floor for object crops, so "elephant in a swimming pool" returns 13 hits and "snow covered ski slope" returns 16. Never demonstrate the empty state from this tab.
  • Thumbnail rate-limiting hits this tab hardest, because it produces the biggest grids. "red car" ungrouped requests 60 thumbnails in one burst against a 120/min shared bucket that one idle tab already drains at 66/min — measured 53 of 60 thumbnails failing with HTTP 429, leaving green boxes drawn on black rectangles. Keep grouping ON (24 cards) for the live demo. Full root-cause analysis is on the Text search page.
  • Scoping to a single camera can inflate the hit count (per-object "red car": 24 across all cameras, 38 when scoped to CAM-12) because the relative relevance floor is computed after camera filtering. Explainable, but do not present the two numbers together unprompted.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • Grouped vs ungrouped per chip: person 12/40, car 17/40, truck 14/40, motorcycle 9/40, bicycle 6/26, bus 8/40, backpack 9/17, handbag 5/10
  • "red car": 24 grouped / 60 shown ungrouped / 78 detections actually pass the relevance filter
  • Top "red car" hit: CAM-14, score 0.3055, track_id 2, label car, 32 frames, 00:00:00–00:00:16 — matches the earlier measurement exactly
  • "red car" spans 7 of the 16 cameras when unscoped
  • 3,362 detections stored, 3,355 with per-crop CLIP embeddings, across 12 distinct YOLO labels
  • Label distribution: person 1,586, car 998, motorcycle 259, truck 240, bus 125, stop sign 47, bicycle 38, backpack 18, traffic light 15, handbag 11, train 8, dog 4

VisionScan / Reference Image search

Upload a still — a witness photo, a screengrab — and CLIP finds the frames that look like it, matching whole-scene composition rather than pixels.

Get there: VisionScan → "Reference Image" tab → click the dashed upload panel → choose a file → Search. Hint reads "Find frames that look like this image (CLIP)".

Show this

  1. A deliberately degraded still — 320 px wide, JPEG quality 45, cropped to 75% — still matches its source camera at 86%.
    Do: Reference Image tab → upload docs/demo-assets/reference_scene_cam14.jpg → Search.
    This is the realistic case: someone hands you a bad phone photo. Upload it and you get back CAM-14 with a 22-frame event spanning 00:00:00–00:00:16. It is not a hash or a pixel diff; the image was re-encoded and re-cropped and it still lands.
  2. Zero false positives across the other 15 cameras.
    Do: Same probe, toggle "Grouping moments" OFF, search again — count the camera labels.
    With grouping OFF the same probe returns 22 hits and every single one is CAM-14, with scores from 0.7224 to 0.86. Out of 417 indexed frames it picked the right camera and nothing else — a clean precision story you can state as a number.
  3. The probe thumbnail renders next to the upload box so the judge sees exactly what was asked for.
    Do: Just upload — the preview appears immediately, before you press Search.
    Small thing, but it makes the demo legible from the back of a room: source image on the left, matches on the right.

If they ask

Is this just image hashing or a pixel diff?
No — it is a CLIP image embedding, the same 512-d space the text queries land in. I proved it by feeding a probe that was cropped to 75%, downscaled to 320 px and re-encoded at JPEG quality 45; a hash would have missed entirely, and it still matched at 0.86. What it matches is scene composition and semantics, not bytes.
Why did the red-car crop return nothing?
Because this tab compares your image against WHOLE FRAMES, and a tight crop of one object has a completely different global composition from the wide street scene it came from. It scored 0.6887 against a 0.72 minimum. That threshold is deliberately high — for reference-image search a false positive is worse than an empty result. The right tool for an object crop is the per-object search on the Object tab, which compares crop-to-crop.
Why is the threshold 0.72 here but 0.22 for text?
Different modalities, different score distributions. Image-to-image CLIP similarity is naturally high — two unrelated street scenes already sit around 0.6 — so a meaningful match has to clear 0.72. Text-to-image similarity is compressed into a much lower band, where 0.3 is already a strong hit. Using one threshold for both would either flood the image results or silence the text results.
What is the false positive rate?
On the one probe I measured: zero. 22 hits returned, all 22 on the correct camera, none of the other 15 cameras or the remaining 395 indexed frames surfaced. That is a single-probe result on a 417-frame index, not a benchmark — I would not quote a general accuracy number from it.
Could an officer upload a suspect's photo here instead of the Face tab?
They could, and they should not. This tab embeds the whole image, so a portrait would match other portrait-like frames by composition rather than identity. Identity matching is the Suspect Face tab, which runs ArcFace on detected faces. I tested exactly this crossover: the face crop uploaded here returns 0 matches, which is the correct refusal.

Careful / known limits

  • The supplied probe reference_red_car.jpg returns 0 matches and will produce a blank screen if used on stage. Measured raw peak similarity 0.6887 against settings.image_min_score = 0.72. This is threshold behaviour working as designed, not a crash, but the file is unusable as a demo asset. I exported a working replacement to the same folder: reference_scene_cam14.jpg (86%, 22 frames, CAM-14 only).
  • Only one probe in the whole dataset was validated for this tab. Reference-image search is the least-exercised of the four modes and I would treat any second probe as unproven until tested — the 0.72 floor means near-misses fail silently to an empty grid with no explanation of why.
  • There is no user-facing explanation when a probe scores below the floor. The screen just reads "0 matches", identical to a genuinely absent subject. An officer cannot tell "not in the footage" from "your crop was too tight".
  • Thumbnail rate-limiting applies here too, though this tab is the least affected because grouped results are usually a single card. Details on the Text search page.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • reference_scene_cam14.jpg: top score 0.86, 22 matching frames, span 00:00:00–00:00:16, all on CAM-14
  • Score range across the 22 hits: 0.7224 to 0.86
  • Cross-camera false positives: 0 out of the other 15 cameras / 395 other indexed frames
  • reference_red_car.jpg: peak raw score 0.6887, below the 0.72 floor → 0 results
  • Probe robustness: matched at 0.86 after 75% crop + downscale to 320 px + JPEG quality 45 (9,135 bytes)
  • Image search latency: 100 ms median warm, including upload and CLIP image encoding (3 runs)
  • Upload validation rejects non-images with HTTP 400 by magic number, not extension

VisionScan / Suspect Face search

Upload one face and ArcFace matches it against 956 indexed face embeddings across all 16 cameras, returning every frame that person appears in as a single timeline event.

Get there: VisionScan → "Suspect Face" tab → upload panel → choose a face image → Search. Hint reads "Match this person across all footage (ArcFace)".

Show this

  1. One face photo, 956 indexed faces, and it finds the right person on the right camera at 84% — with nothing on the other 15 cameras.
    Do: Suspect Face tab → upload docs/demo-assets/suspect_face.jpg → Search → click the card to open the 13-frame gallery.
    The probe returns 13 frames, every one on CAM-07, spanning 00:00:00–00:00:14. There are 262 indexed faces on other cameras and not one of them cleared the threshold. That is the single cleanest precision result in the module.
  2. The result collapses into one card that reads like a movement timeline: 13 frames, 00:00:00–00:00:14.
    Do: Click the result card. The gallery strip and "Add all to report" both work here (unlike per-object results).
    This is the investigative answer, not a search result — "this person was in view of this camera for 14 seconds and here is every frame". Opening it gives all 13 frames with a working "Add all to report" button straight into a PDF.
  3. It refuses cleanly when there is no face.
    Do: Upload docs/demo-assets/reference_red_car.jpg into the Suspect Face tab → 0 matches.
    Feed it a photo of a car and it returns 0 matches rather than a nearest-neighbour guess. On a policing tool, a system that declines is more credible than one that always answers.
  4. It found the highest-quality face available in the entire dataset — I checked.
    Do: No action — this is a claim you can make about the probe.
    The supplied probe is the top-ranked face by detector confidence across all 956 (det_score 0.922, CAM-07 @ 13.0 s). I queried the whole faces table looking for something better and there is nothing above it, so this is the strongest demo the data supports.

If they ask

What model is doing the face matching, and why not CLIP?
ArcFace, via InsightFace, run at ingestion over every detected face — 956 embeddings stored. CLIP is trained for semantic similarity and would happily match two different people who look alike in context; ArcFace is trained specifically for identity with an angular margin loss, so its embedding space separates individuals rather than scenes. They are stored in separate FAISS indexes and never mixed.
84% match — 84% of what? Is that a probability that it is the same person?
No, and I would not let anyone read it that way. It is cosine similarity in ArcFace embedding space rendered as a percentage. Our accept threshold is 0.30. The honest interpretation is ranking plus a floor, not calibrated probability — we have not run a ROC on this dataset, so I cannot give you a false-accept rate. For a real deployment that calibration is mandatory before anyone acts on a match.
How do you know these 13 hits are the same person and not 13 lookalikes?
Strictly speaking I do not have ground-truth identity labels, so I can only give you the evidence: all 13 hits are on one camera within a 14-second window, and 262 indexed faces on the other cameras — including 87 on CAM-06 and 73 on CAM-12 — scored below threshold. That is consistent with one person walking through one frame. It is not proof, and the PDF report says as much on every page.
This is face recognition on public CCTV. What stops it being abused?
Three things, and I will be honest that they are the beginning rather than the end. The report footer stamps every exported PDF with a SHA-256 and the line "Frames are AI-ranked candidates and should be verified by an investigator before use as evidence" — the tool never asserts identity. The platform is role-gated so a citizen account cannot reach this screen. And the search is per-query and logged, not a standing watchlist. What is genuinely missing is a warrant/authorisation gate and a retention policy on the face index — I would not deploy this without both.
Why is face search 560 ms when text is 80 ms?
Because the probe has to be processed first: detect the face in the uploaded image, align it, then run the ArcFace encoder, before the FAISS lookup over 956 vectors even starts. The lookup itself is negligible. Text search only has to encode a short string.
Only 956 faces from 417 frames — is the face detector missing people?
It is finding what is findable. 694 of those 956 are on CAM-07, the close-range market camera; the overhead traffic cameras contribute almost none because faces are a handful of pixels at that distance and angle. That is an honest property of the camera geometry, not a tuning problem — CCTV mounted for vehicle coverage will not give you usable faces, which is why the Scene Analytics screen reports per-camera quality separately.

Careful / known limits

  • The results header reads "1 matches for [face:suspect_face.jpg]" when grouping is ON. It is correct — one event — but it sounds like a weak result if read aloud, and the plural/singular is ungrammatical. Either toggle grouping OFF for 13 cards, or narrate the badge instead of the header.
  • The matched thumbnail is a wide crowded-market frame in which the suspect is small and hard to see at card size. Unlike per-object search there is no bounding box drawn around the matched face, so from the audience it is not visually obvious what matched. Open the detail modal to make the point, or be ready to explain.
  • No calibrated confidence. The 0.30 accept threshold is a hand-set constant with no ROC or false-accept-rate analysis behind it on this dataset. If a judge asks for accuracy, say so plainly rather than quoting the 84% as an accuracy figure.
  • Only one identity was ever validated. All my testing used the single best probe; I have no evidence about how the system behaves on a harder face (profile, motion-blurred, partially occluded). Do not claim general face-recognition performance.
  • Thumbnail rate-limiting applies, though this tab is minimally affected with grouping ON (1 card). Opening the 13-frame gallery requests 13 more thumbnails at once, which can break the gallery strip if the bucket is already drained. Full analysis on the Text search page.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 956 face embeddings indexed across 16 cameras (694 CAM-07, 87 CAM-06, 73 CAM-12, 40 CAM-08, 39 CAM-13, 19 CAM-15, 2 CAM-14, 1 CAM-10, 1 CAM-02)
  • Probe result: top score 0.8428 (84%), 13 matching frames, span 00:00:00–00:00:14, all on CAM-07
  • Cross-camera false positives: 0 — none of the 262 faces indexed on other cameras cleared the 0.30 threshold
  • Probe quality: det_score 0.922, the highest-confidence face of all 956 in the dataset (next best 0.907)
  • Face search latency: 560 ms median warm (3 runs), the slowest of the four search modes
  • Accept threshold: face_min_score = 0.30 cosine similarity in ArcFace space
  • Negative control: a car crop uploaded to this tab returns 0 matches

VisionScan / Scene analytics + PDF report

Per-camera tracking analytics with an honest self-assessed quality grade, draw-on-frame zone and tripwire tools, and a branded forensic PDF with a chain-of-custody hash.

Get there: Scene analytics: VisionScan → left rail "Camera Feeds" → the "Scene analytics" button on any of the 16 feed cards. PDF report: select frames on any result card (top-left square) or via "Add all to report" in a detail modal → "Generate Report" in the bottom tray → "Download PDF".

Show this

  1. It tells you it collapsed 251 raw detections into 45 real people — and grades how much it trusts itself.
    Do: Left rail → CAM-07 → "Scene analytics". Hover the quality chip to show the tooltip.
    CAM-07 shows "45 distinct objects" beside a struck-through "251 raw detections" and a green "5.6× fewer once tracked" badge, then a TRACKING QUALITY chip reading "good" with a tooltip that explains exactly why: ~2.0 fps keyframes, 13% of detections seen only once or twice. A system that publishes its own error mode is far more credible than one that does not.
  2. The same screen grades CAM-01 "poor" and explains that 100% of its detections fragmented.
    Do: Open CAM-07 (good, 5.6×), close, open CAM-01 (poor, 1.0×, 100% fragmented).
    This is the moment that wins technical judges. The tool volunteers that one of its own cameras is unusable — 7 detections, 7 tracks, ~0.2 fps, 100% fragmentation — instead of presenting a confident number. Show CAM-07 (good) and CAM-01 (poor) back to back.
  3. Draw a polygon on a real CCTV frame and get occupancy over time; draw a line and get directional footfall.
    Do: CAM-12 → Scene analytics → Zone → click 4 corners → "Analyse zone". Then Line → two points across the lower third → "Count crossings".
    Click four points on the crowded-market frame, hit "Analyse zone" → peak occupancy 7 at 5.96 s, mean 2.9, 95 distinct objects entered, plus a sparkline. Switch to Line, click twice, "Count crossings" → 16 in / 18 out with a per-event list naming track IDs and timestamps. Nothing is pre-configured; the judge picks the area.
  4. Selected frames export to a branded forensic PDF stamped with a reproducible SHA-256.
    Do: Search "crowded market street" → click a card → "Add all to report" → "Generate Report" → set the case title → "Download PDF".
    The PDF carries a CityShield header, a CONFIDENTIAL band, case title, investigator auto-filled from the logged-in officer, and per-frame camera / timestamp / frame ID / source filename / object inventory — with the thumbnails embedded. The footer reads "Integrity SHA-256: 5b07…6223". Rendering is two-pass and deterministic, so the same content re-renders to the same hash.
  5. The report can be filed against a real open case, and the whole search hands off to FIR drafting in one click.
    Do: Open the report modal to show the case dropdown; then back on results, click "Draft FIR in Arbiter".
    The report modal has a "Link to one of your cases" dropdown listing live cases (#29 Digital-arrest cyber-fraud ring, #35 Loan-app extortion network, …), and the results header has "Draft FIR in Arbiter" which jumps modules with the incident pre-written. The CCTV search is wired into the actual case workflow, not a standalone toy.

If they ask

What does "5.6× fewer once tracked" actually mean?
CAM-07 has 251 person detections across its keyframes but only 45 distinct tracks. Every keyframe re-detects the same people, so the raw count massively overstates footfall — tracking links those boxes into one identity each. The 5.6× is the inflation factor you would have reported if you had naively counted detections. We show both numbers and the ratio precisely so nobody quotes the wrong one.
Your own screen says CAM-12 tracking quality is "poor". Why should I trust the 95 objects it just counted?
You should trust it less than the CAM-07 number, and that is exactly why the badge is there. Poor means 43% of detections were seen only once or twice, so the tracker is fragmenting one object into several — which inflates "distinct objects" upward. The direction of the error is knowable: on a poor-graded camera, distinct-object counts are an over-estimate and the true figure is lower. We would rather hand an investigator a graded number than a confident wrong one.
How is the quality grade computed?
Two inputs, both shown in the tooltip: keyframe density (fps estimate) and measured fragmentation — the share of detections belonging to tracks seen only once or twice. CAM-07 is good at ~2.0 fps and 13% fragmentation; CAM-01 is poor at ~0.2 fps and 100% fragmentation, because it only has 3 keyframes so every detection is its own track. It is a heuristic, not a learned metric, and it is deliberately pessimistic.
Is the tripwire counting people or detections?
Tracks. A crossing is registered when a track's bottom-centre point moves from one side of the line to the other, so one person walking through counts once regardless of how many keyframes they appear in. The event list names the track ID, which is why you see the same #11 cross out and then back in — that is one person doubling back, not two people.
Why does the crossing count change so much when I move the line?
Because it only counts tracks that actually cross that line, and the footage has strong movement corridors. On CAM-10 a line at 30% height catches 31 crossings; the same line at 60% catches 9. That is real geometry, not instability — but it does mean an operator has to place the line where the traffic is, which is why the tool is interactive rather than automatic.
What makes the PDF forensically useful rather than just a printout?
Four things. Every frame carries its camera, timestamp, frame ID and source filename, so it is traceable back to the original video. The object inventory per frame is recorded. The footer carries a SHA-256 of the document content, rendered two-pass so the hash is reproducible — re-render the same content and you get the same digest, which means tampering is detectable. And the footer states that frames are AI-ranked candidates requiring investigator verification, so the document never overstates itself.
Can I verify that hash independently?
Yes in principle and with one caveat you should know about. The two-pass render pins the timestamp and turns on ReportLab's invariant flag, so identical content plus identical timestamp produces byte-identical output — I confirmed that by generating the same report twice and getting the same digest. The caveat is that the documented X-Integrity-SHA256 response header is not currently emitted by the endpoint, so today you verify against the value printed in the footer rather than against a header.
Is the report linked to a case, or is it a loose file?
The report modal has a "Link to one of your cases" dropdown populated with the officer's live cases, so the export can be filed against case #29, #35 and so on rather than floating free. And from the search results, "Draft FIR in Arbiter" carries the camera, timestamp and hit count into the FIR drafting module. The CCTV search is a step in the case workflow, not a separate tool.

Careful / known limits

  • /api/videos/{id}/zone-analytics and /api/videos/{id}/line-crossings have no auth dependency at all. Every other VisionScan route takes Depends(auth_gate); these two in backend/app/api/zone_routes.py take none. Confirmed by calling both with no token and getting HTTP 200. This is invisible today because VISIONSCAN_REQUIRE_AUTH=false, but flipping that flag to true would secure every route EXCEPT these two. Worth fixing after the demo, not before.
  • The X-Integrity-SHA256 response header is never sent. build_report_with_integrity() returns the digest and integrity_headers() exists to serve it, but /api/report calls the plain build_report() and sets only Content-Disposition — the function has no caller outside report.py. The hash is still stamped in the PDF footer, so the feature works, but do not claim the API exposes it.
  • A report requested with nonexistent frame IDs returns HTTP 200 and a PDF that says "Frames in report: 1" while rendering no frames, because the count is len(frame_ids) rather than the number actually resolved. Cosmetic, but it is a forensic document making a false count claim.
  • PDF timestamps are in the container's clock/timezone, not IST. My reports generated just after midnight IST on 20 Aug are stamped "2026-08-19 19:53:48". For an evidence document the timezone matters; a judge reading the date could reasonably query it.
  • Zone analytics for a nonexistent video ID returns HTTP 200 with empty results instead of 404 (tracks endpoint correctly returns 404). Minor inconsistency.
  • The report request has no user-facing error handling. When it was rate-limited the UI threw an uncaught 'Request failed with status code 429' page error and the modal simply hung — no message, no retry. If the PDF button appears dead on stage, that is why; wait 30 seconds and click again.
  • DO NOT click "Re-index" in the Camera Feeds header. It queues a full re-ingestion of all 16 videos on a CPU-only backend and would disrupt everything mid-demo.
  • "1.0× fewer once tracked" on CAM-01 is a confusing way to say "no compression at all". Minor copy issue on a screen you may deliberately show.
  • Thumbnail rate-limiting affects the Scene Analytics drawing canvas too — it loads one keyframe, which can 429 and leave the dashed "frame unavailable" placeholder, making the zone/line tools undrawable. If that happens, close and reopen the panel after ~30 seconds. Full root-cause analysis on the Text search page.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • Per-camera tracking (distinct objects / raw detections / quality / fragmentation): CAM-07 45/251 good 13%; CAM-14 37/107 degraded 31%; CAM-10 132/441 degraded 25%; CAM-12 261/551 poor 43%; CAM-11 48/59 poor 80%; CAM-01 7/7 poor 100%
  • Detection-to-object inflation factors shown on screen: CAM-07 5.6×, CAM-10 3.3×, CAM-12 2.1×, CAM-01 1.0×
  • 1,409 distinct tracks across 3,362 detections platform-wide
  • Zone occupancy CAM-12 (centre polygon): peak 7 at 5.96 s, mean 2.9, 95 distinct objects entered
  • Zone occupancy CAM-07 (same polygon): peak 3 at 7.5 s, mean 0.5, 7 distinct objects entered
  • Line crossings, best placement per camera: CAM-12 34 (16 in/18 out), CAM-10 31 (16/15), CAM-13 17 (8/9), CAM-07 10 (5/5), CAM-14 4 (1/3)

  • PDF: 4 frames → 1 page / 232 KB; 6 frames → 1 page / 337 KB; 24 frames → 4 pages / 1.19 MB, all thumbnails embedded as JPEG
  • PDF generation latency: 140 ms server-side for a 6-frame report
  • PDF re-render is byte-identical for identical content and timestamp (verified over two consecutive requests)

VisionScan — Camera Feeds rail: Upload, Live Feed, Delete feed

The ingestion half of VisionScan — file upload, and a live-stream mode with three pre-wired public HLS feeds that captures and indexes a stream in real time. Entirely untested in the coverage document, and it contains the two most destructive buttons in the product.

Get there: VisionScan → left rail "Camera Feeds". Header has a "Re-index" button and a feed count. Below it a two-tab strip: "Upload" (default) / "Live Feed". Each of the 16 feed cards carries a "Scene analytics" button AND a "Delete feed" button.

Show this

  1. A "Live Feed" tab that takes an RTSP / HLS / YouTube-live URL and either goes live (watch and search a stream while it is being indexed) or captures a bounded window — with three verified public streams one click away.
    Do: VisionScan → Camera Feeds → "Live Feed" tab. READ THE ETHICS LINE OUT LOUD, show the three catalogued feeds, and describe it. Only actually click Go Live if you have accepted the trade-off in `broken` below.
    It answers the obvious judge question "this only works on files you uploaded" with a live stream. The catalogue is deliberately built around ethics: the panel prints "Use only intentionally-public feeds (govt traffic cams, public live streams). Accessing private/unsecured cameras without authorization is illegal." and the backend enforces an SSRF/public-address guard for non-admin callers.
  2. The catalogue is overridable without touching code — drop a public_feeds.json into the data directory to paste in a real Gujarat traffic-camera URL before demo day.
    It is the honest answer to "could you run this on our cameras tomorrow?": one JSON file, no rebuild.
  3. Each feed card carries its own keyframe budget, which makes the per-camera quality story legible before you even open Scene Analytics: CAM-12 60 keyframes / 28s, CAM-01 only 3 keyframes / 13s.
    Pairs directly with the Scene Analytics quality badge — CAM-01 is graded 'poor' precisely because it only yielded 3 keyframes, and that number is visible on the rail.

If they ask

Everything you have shown is pre-ingested footage. Can it watch a live camera?
Yes, and the control is on this screen — the Live Feed tab takes an RTSP, HLS or YouTube-live URL and there are two modes: capture a bounded window, or go live and index continuously while you watch and search the same stream. We ship three intentionally-public streams so it demonstrates itself out of the box. I have verified all three are reachable from inside the backend container right now. What we have not done is point it at a real Gujarat traffic camera — that is a URL, not an engineering change.
What stops someone pointing this at a private camera?
Three things, and the first is a policy statement printed on the panel itself rather than buried in docs. Technically, a non-admin caller goes through a public-address assertion on the URL before anything is stored, and because a YouTube link can resolve to a private address, the resolved stream URL is re-validated inside the worker as well. An authenticated admin is trusted to ingest an internal LAN feed, which is the correct behaviour for a station operating its own cameras. What we do not have is a warrant or authorisation gate — that is a real gap for deployment.
If you go live, does the anomaly watch fire?
That is exactly the difference. The auto-case and dispatch loop is gated on the feed being LIVE — uploads of historical footage deliberately never notify or auto-create a case, so reviewing old evidence cannot spam the control room. All 16 seeded feeds are is_live=false, which is why Live Alerts is empty. A live feed is the only way to demonstrate the full detection→case→dispatch loop on this data.
Why do the cameras have such different keyframe counts?
Because keyframes are sampled at roughly 2 fps against clip length and content, and the source clips differ. CAM-12 is 28 seconds and yields 60 keyframes; CAM-01 is 13 seconds and yields only 3, which is exactly why Scene Analytics grades CAM-01 'poor' with 100% track fragmentation. The rail shows the raw budget so the quality grade downstream is not a surprise.

Careful / known limits

  • "Delete feed" sits on every one of the 16 camera cards, always visible, directly beside the "Scene analytics" button the presenter is expected to click repeatedly during the demo. I found no confirmation step. One slip removes a camera from the demo. This is the highest-probability destructive misclick in the product and it was not in the coverage document at all.
  • Going live or capturing once BOTH insert a new videos row before any processing, so either one permanently makes it 17 feeds. That collides head-on with the dashboard headline "16 camera feeds, 417 indexed frames" and with the Live Alerts recommendation to ingest a fire clip. You can have at most ONE of: round dashboard numbers, a live-stream demo, or a populated Live Alerts screen. Decide before 9am, not on stage.
  • The three catalogued live feeds are public INTERNET URLs (mux.dev, unified-streaming.com, akamaihd.net). They resolve tonight from both the host and the container, but if the venue Wi-Fi is captive-portalled or blocked, clicking Go Live fails in front of the judges. The rest of the platform is genuinely offline; this one feature is not.
  • Even when a live feed is capturing correctly, the in-page preview can show "preview unsupported" — the player only renders .m3u8 natively, so an RTSP or YouTube URL is still being indexed on the backend but shows a warning triangle instead of video. If you improvise a non-HLS URL on stage it will look broken while actually working.
  • "Re-index" in the rail header, as previously reported: queues a full re-ingestion of all 16 videos on a CPU-only backend. Do not click it.
  • The camera rail is ordered by created_at DESC (CAM-16, 14, 15, 13, 12, 10, 11, 09…), as previously reported. Cosmetic.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 16 feeds, all status 'ready', all is_live=false — confirmed against GET /api/videos
  • Keyframes per feed sum to exactly 417, matching the INDEXED FRAMES KPI
  • 3 catalogued public live feeds; all 3 returned HTTP 200 from the host (0.20s / 1.16s / 1.56s) and from inside the backend container (0.08s / 0.32s)
  • 16 always-visible "Delete feed" buttons, with no confirmation step observed
  • File upload accepts video/* only

PDF investigation reports — VisionScan report export

A one-click forensic PDF that turns selected CCTV search hits into a branded, chain-of-custody-stamped investigation report — SHA-256 sealed via a deterministic two-pass render, in under a quarter of a second.

Get there: Sign in as staff → VisionScan → run a search → tick the checkbox on the frames you want → floating tray at the bottom → 'Generate Report' → optionally link a case → 'Download PDF'

Show this

  1. Tick frames from a live search and a floating tray appears; two clicks later a branded 'CCTV Investigation Report' PDF downloads. 10 frames → 2 pages, 538.7 KB, in 0.21 seconds.
    Do: VisionScan → search 'red car' → click 'Find every object' (24 results) → tick 10 checkboxes → 'Generate Report' → 'Download PDF'.
    The gap between 'the AI found something' and 'I have a document I can put in a case file' is where most demos stop. This closes it in two clicks, and the speed means you can do it live without dead air.
  2. Every page footer carries 'Integrity SHA-256: 7589…8585 · generated 2026-08-19 20:13:02' plus 'CONFIDENTIAL — For authorized investigation use only'. The hash comes from a two-pass render: pass 1 hashes the content, pass 2 re-renders with that hash stamped into the footer.
    Do: Open the PDF → scroll to any page footer → read the Integrity line. If a judge pushes, mention it's a two-pass render with rl_config.invariant = 1.
    Naive self-hashing is impossible — stamping the hash changes the bytes. The two-pass trick with ReportLab's invariant flag (which pins internal PDF timestamps and IDs) makes the document bytes a pure function of its content, so the printed hash is genuinely re-verifiable. I proved it: two identical requests produced byte-identical 165,781-byte PDFs with the same digest.
  3. Per frame, the report prints the provenance a court would ask for: 'CAM-14 @ 00:00:16', 'Frame #2236 · traffic_redcar_06.mp4', and the full YOLO object inventory 'Objects: car(16), person(3), truck(2), motorcycle(1)'.
    Do: Point at any thumbnail caption block in the PDF.
    It ties every thumbnail back to a specific camera, a specific timecode, a specific source file and a specific frame index — and it publishes what the detector saw, not just what the operator was looking for. That is auditable.
  4. The dialog offers your open cases and auto-titles the report from the one you pick — selecting '#29 · Digital-arrest cyber-fraud ring busted' fills the title as 'Case #29 · Digital-arrest cyber-fraud ring busted', which also becomes the PDF's document title metadata.
    Do: In the Generate Report dialog, open the 'Link case' dropdown and pick #29 — watch the title field fill itself.
    The report is filed against real casework rather than being an orphan download, and the investigator field pre-fills with the signed-in officer's name and badge number — 'Station Admin (ADM-001)'.
  5. The report ends with an honest disclaimer: 'Frames are AI-ranked candidates and should be verified by an investigator before use as evidence.'
    Do: Scroll to the last line of the final page.
    It is the sentence that makes the whole thing deployable. The team is claiming a search tool that narrows 417 indexed frames to 10, not an oracle that decides guilt — and they put that in writing on the artefact itself.
