If they ask

What does the SHA-256 in the footer actually protect against?
Alteration of an issued document. It is a two-pass render: pass 1 builds the PDF with a blank stamp and hashes those bytes, pass 2 rebuilds the same content with that digest printed in the footer. ReportLab runs with rl_config.invariant = 1 so internal PDF timestamps and object IDs are pinned, which makes the byte stream a pure function of the content — I verified two identical requests produce byte-identical files. Be precise about the claim though: the 'Generated' timestamp is part of the hashed content, so re-rendering the same frames three seconds later gives a different hash. It seals a specific issued document; it is not a content-only fingerprint of the frames.
Could someone regenerate the PDF, change a frame, and re-stamp a matching hash?
Yes, if they control the server — the hash is self-computed, not signed by an external authority. This is tamper-evidence, not non-repudiation. Making it non-repudiable means signing the digest with a key the station doesn't hold, or anchoring it to an external timestamp authority. Neither is implemented. What it does buy you is that a PDF edited after download will no longer match its own footer.
Are the AI's bounding boxes in the report?
No, and I'd rather say so than get caught. On screen the green box is a CSS overlay positioned from normalised coordinates the API returns as match_bbox — that's why the on-screen result looks so precise. The PDF embeds the raw keyframe, so the box isn't there. What the PDF does carry instead is the full YOLO object inventory per frame in text — 'Objects: car(16), person(3), truck(2), motorcycle(1)' — so the detection evidence is present, just not drawn.
Why is the PDF creation date the year 2000?
That's ReportLab's invariant mode, and it's deliberate. Fixed internal timestamps are exactly what makes the byte output reproducible and therefore the integrity hash verifiable. The real generation time is printed in the document body and in every page footer. It does look odd in File > Properties, and while we were in there we also left author and subject as '(anonymous)' / '(unspecified)' even though we know the investigator's name — that's a genuine miss, one line to fix.
You mention an X-Integrity-SHA256 header — I don't see it.
You're right, it isn't emitted. The report service defines build_report_with_integrity() returning (bytes, digest) and an integrity_headers() helper specifically for this, but the route at backend/app/api/routes.py:393 still calls the older build_report(), which returns bytes only. So the footer stamp is correct and the machine-readable header is missing. It's a two-line fix at the serving site that nobody made after the integrity work landed.
How big does this get with a realistic number of frames?
Roughly linear at about 50 KB per embedded keyframe: 1 frame is 51,816 bytes on one page, 10 frames is 551,641 bytes on two pages, six thumbnails to a page. So a 50-frame report would be around 2.5 MB and 9 pages. Generation is fast enough not to matter — 0.04s for one frame, 0.21s for ten — because the keyframes are already extracted and on disk; the report step is layout, not inference.
What happens if the PDF fails to generate during the demo?
Nothing visible, which is the problem. App.jsx:255 has no catch and ReportTray's generate() uses try/finally without one, so a rejected request just stops the spinner and leaves the dialog open with no message. The presenter would be standing there clicking a button that appears to do nothing. Everything I ran returned 200, but that's the failure mode if it happens.
Can a citizen generate one of these?
The button lives inside VisionScan, which citizens have no nav entry for, and the search and frame endpoints are staff-gated. I did not find a way to reach the report dialog as a citizen. I'd note the /report route itself sits behind a general auth gate rather than an explicit staff-role check, so I would want that tightened before this went anywhere near production.
Why does the report timestamp not match the clock in the room?
Container clock skew — the backend is on UTC and the demo is in IST. When my host clock read 01:43 the report stamped 20:13 the previous day, a 5h30m difference. On a chain-of-custody document that is worth fixing before anyone deploys it, because 'generated at' on an evidence artefact should match the wall clock of the station that generated it.

Careful / known limits

  • The green bounding box is NOT in the PDF. It is a CSS overlay in FrameCard.jsx drawn from match_bbox; the report embeds the raw keyframe. Do not tell judges the box is in the report — show the box on screen and the object inventory in the PDF.
  • X-Integrity-SHA256 response header is documented in backend/app/services/report.py but never emitted, because backend/app/api/routes.py:393 calls build_report() instead of build_report_with_integrity(). The in-PDF footer stamp is fine.
  • Clock skew: the container is on UTC while the presenter is on IST, so the report's 'Generated' line is 5h30m behind the room. Measured: PDF stamped 2026-08-19 20:13:02 while the host clock read 2026-08-20 01:43. On a chain-of-custody document a judge may well ask about this.
  • A nonexistent frame id (e.g. 999999) returns HTTP 200 with a 2,378-byte near-empty PDF rather than an error. Silent success on bad input.
  • No error feedback if generation fails: App.jsx:255 generateReport has no catch, ReportTray.generate has try/finally with no catch. A failed export shows the user absolutely nothing — the dialog just stops spinning.
  • PDF document metadata is half-filled: title is correct, but author='(anonymous)', subject='(unspecified)', creator='(unspecified)' even though the investigator's name and badge are known and printed in the body.
  • The TOP-ranked hit for 'red car' (Frame #2236, CAM-14 @ 00:00:16) is visually ambiguous — a red shape partly behind a blue box. If you build the report from the top hit alone, the flagship thumbnail is unconvincing. Tick the CAM-14 hits at 00:00:12 and 00:00:00 instead; those are unmistakable red cars.
  • The integrity hash is not reproducible across time, only within the same generation second, because the 'Generated' timestamp is hashed as part of the content. Do not claim 'same frames always give the same hash' — claim 'this document has not been altered since it was issued'.
  • POST /api/report with query_type 'region' returns a 422 whose body is an array of objects. The UI never sends it, but any 422 that does reach the client is the crash pattern described on the Login page.
  • The /report route is behind a general auth gate rather than an explicit staff-role check. I could not reach it as a citizen through the UI, but I would not rely on that alone.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 1-frame report: 1 page, 51,816 bytes (50.6 KB), 1 embedded image, 0.04s to generate
  • 10-frame report: 2 pages, 551,641 bytes (538.7 KB), 10 embedded images (6 on page 1, 4 on page 2), 0.21s to generate
  • Roughly 50 KB per embedded keyframe; 6 thumbnails per page
  • Reproducibility check: two identical requests produced byte-identical 165,781-byte PDFs with the same SHA-256
  • Source corpus behind the reports: 16 camera feeds, 417 indexed keyframes
  • Search feeding the report: 'red car' text mode → 1 hit (CAM-14, score 0.301); region/object mode → 24 hits (top 4 scores 0.305, 0.300, 0.294, 0.294)
  • API search latency warm: 0.1s; PDF generation 0.04–0.21s
  • Report clock skew vs the room: 5 hours 30 minutes (container UTC vs IST)
  • Empty frame_ids → clean HTTP 400; bogus frame id → HTTP 200 with a 2,378-byte empty report

Live Alerts (Anomaly Watch)

The always-on anomaly watch — fire, smoke, accident, weapon and violence detection running over the same keyframe stream the search index uses — surfaced as acknowledgeable alert cards with a detection box drawn on the frame. RIGHT NOW IT IS COMPLETELY EMPTY AND WILL SHOW "All clear — no active alerts." ON STAGE.

Get there: Log in as admin@city.gov / admin123, then click "Live Alerts" in the top nav (3rd item). The header siren icon also jumps here.

Show this

  1. The architecture, not the screen: anomaly detection costs ZERO extra inference. Every keyframe already gets a CLIP vector for search; the watch scores that same existing vector against five per-class prompt ensembles, and separately scans the YOLO detections the pipeline already produced.
    Do: With the empty state on screen, read the intro line the page already shows: "The anomaly watch scans every feed for fire, smoke, accidents, weapons and violence — incidents are pinned here the moment they are seen." Then explain it reuses the search embedding. Do not promise to show an alert unless you have ingested a fire clip first (see inputs).
    It is genuinely clever engineering and it is the honest thing to talk about while the list is empty. No second model pass, no second GPU budget — anomaly detection is a nearly free by-product of building the search index. That is the answer to "can this scale to 5,000 city cameras?"
  2. The false-positive guard is a measured margin, not a raw threshold. A class only fires when it beats the best "normal scene" prompt by a calibrated margin — six negative prompts ("a normal city street with traffic", "calm traffic flowing on a road", …) anchor it.
    Do: Say: "sixteen live feeds, four hundred and seventeen frames analysed, zero false alarms. That empty list is the result, not a bug." It is true and it is measured.
    This is exactly why the screen is empty, and the empty state is a TRUE NEGATIVE worth claiming: 16 cameras of ordinary Indian street, market and traffic footage — 417 keyframes, 3,362 object detections — produced zero false alarms. A detector that screams on normal traffic is worse than no detector in a control room.
  3. The closed loop behind the card: a NEW event on a LIVE feed auto-spawns a geo-tagged case and dispatches the nearest patrol unit — the card then renders a "CASE #n AUTO-CREATED" chip that deep-links into the case workspace.
    Do: Only describe this — you cannot show it without a live feed producing an alert. The code path is services/anomaly_events.py → incident_loop.handle_new_event(), gated on is_live and the VISIONSCAN_AUTO_CASE flag, and fail-soft so it can never block ingestion.
    Detection → case → dispatch with no human in the loop is the differentiator over a CCTV analytics box. The nearest-unit pick is real: it haversines each officer's most recent patrol check-in area against the incident area.
  4. Debouncing: a burning car produces dozens of consecutive fire keyframes, and the operator gets ONE alert that grows a frame counter, not a flood.
    Do: Describe it while pointing at where the "N frames" badge would be. Or show the stubbed screenshot at docs/demo-assets/shots/livealerts_cards_stubbed.png if you want a visual.
    Anyone who has run a real control room knows alert fatigue kills the system. Signals are grouped per (video_id, type) within a 30-second gap; the grouped event keeps the MAX confidence and increments peak_count, which the card renders as "7 frames".

If they ask

Why is this screen empty? Is it broken?
It is empty because nothing anomalous has happened. The DB has zero anomaly_events, and that is the correct output: all 16 loaded clips are ordinary Indian street, market and traffic CCTV — there is no fire, no weapon and no violence in any of them. 417 keyframes were scored and none crossed the margin. I would rather show you a detector that stays quiet on normal footage than one that fires on a red car.
So you can't actually show me a detection?
Not on this data — be straight about it. We can ingest a fire clip live (test_clips/fire/) and it will appear here, or you can look at the fire/smoke detection running inside the VisionScan search module. What this screen proves right now is the false-positive rate on 16 real feeds, which is zero.
How does the detection work? Is it a separate model per class?
No — it is hybrid and costs zero extra inference. Every keyframe already gets a CLIP vector for semantic search. The anomaly watch scores that SAME existing vector against five prompt ensembles (fire, smoke, accident, weapon, violence), each averaged over three phrasings so one odd wording cannot dominate. In parallel it scans the YOLO detections the pipeline already produced for weapon-class labels — COCO 'knife', plus a specialized fire/smoke weights file if present. Neither path adds a model pass.
CLIP zero-shot on CCTV frames will fire on anything orange. How do you stop false positives?
A measured margin, not a raw threshold. A class fires only when it beats the BEST of six negative 'normal scene' prompts — 'a normal city street with traffic', 'calm traffic flowing on a road', 'people walking peacefully on a sidewalk', and so on — by a calibrated margin (default 0.03, with per-class overrides, calibrated empirically via scripts/anomaly_selftest.py). The margin is what keeps ordinary street footage at effectively zero, and the empty screen in front of you is that calibration working on 417 real frames.
Why do some alerts have a box drawn and others don't?
Because only YOLO signals are localised. CLIP scores the WHOLE frame — it tells you the scene looks like a fire, not where in the frame the fire is — so a CLIP-sourced alert has null coordinates and the card deliberately draws no box rather than inventing one. YOLO signals carry a real bounding box, stored as 0..1 frame fractions so it renders correctly at any thumbnail size. I verified both paths: a YOLO stub with x1=0.25,y1=0.30,x2=0.62,y2=0.78 drew a box at exactly left 25% / top 30% / width 37% / height 48%, and a CLIP stub with null coordinates drew nothing.
What stops a burning car generating a hundred alerts?
Debouncing per (video_id, type). A new signal joins the most recent open event of the same type on the same feed if its last grouped frame is within 30 seconds; otherwise it opens a new event. The grouped event keeps the MAX confidence seen, bumps a peak_count, and the card renders that as 'N frames'. One incident, one card that grows — not a flood.
What happens after an alert fires? Does someone have to notice it?
No. A NEWLY created event on a LIVE feed above the notify threshold (0.70) pings the admins, and separately triggers the incident loop: it auto-creates a geo-tagged case and dispatches the nearest patrol unit — nearest computed by haversining each officer's most recent patrol check-in area against the incident area, with a fallback to any active officer so dispatch never silently no-ops. The card then shows a 'CASE #n AUTO-CREATED' chip that deep-links into the case workspace. Uploads of historical footage deliberately do NOT notify or auto-case, so reviewing old evidence does not spam the control room.
What are the five classes and why those?
fire, smoke, accident, weapon, violence. They are the five that (a) a city CCTV operator is actually watching for and (b) CLIP can separate from a normal street scene with a prompt ensemble. Note fire and smoke are separate classes on purpose — smoke usually precedes visible flame, so it is the earlier warning.
How fast would an alert appear?
It is written during ingestion, so within one keyframe interval of the frame being processed, and the UI polls every 5 seconds — I measured 4 poll requests in a 21-second window. The bottleneck is keyframe extraction rate, not the detector.
Can an operator suppress a camera that keeps false-alarming?
Not per-camera today, honestly. There are global and per-class confidence thresholds via environment variables (anomaly_conf_threshold, anomaly_class_thresholds, anomaly_weapon_yolo_conf) and an operator can dismiss individual alerts, which is audited — but there is no per-camera mute or zone mask in the UI. That is a real gap for a production control room.

Careful / known limits

  • THE SCREEN IS EMPTY AND WILL BE EMPTY ON STAGE. Verified three ways: anomaly_events has 0 rows in the DB, all three status tabs return [] from the API, and /api/anomalies/summary returns {"new":0,"total":0}. Nothing is wrong with the code — the 16 loaded clips simply contain no anomalies. If you want a card on screen you must ingest test_clips/fire/fire_highrise_shanghai_01.ogv BEFORE the demo, which will change FEEDS 16→17 and INDEXED FRAMES 417→~440 on the dashboard. Decide which you care about more; do not discover this at 9am.
  • I COULD NOT VERIFY AN END-TO-END REAL DETECTION. Everything I report about the card layout, the box overlay geometry, the LIVE pill, the frame counter and the auto-created-case chip was verified by intercepting /api/anomalies in the browser and serving synthetic records in the exact shape the backend returns. The RENDERING path is proven correct; the DETECTION path is not proven on this data by me. Treat that distinction as load-bearing.
  • A SINGLE 429 REPLACES THE WHOLE LIST WITH AN ERROR. I hit "Could not load alerts. Retrying every 5s…" on the Acknowledged tab purely from rate limiting. Because this view polls every 5 seconds it is the heaviest per-minute consumer of the shared 120 req/min bucket after the health/videos pollers, so it is the most likely screen to trip it. It does self-heal on the next poll.
  • No per-camera mute, no zone masking, no threshold control in the UI. Everything is environment-variable configuration. Do not offer to "tune it live".
  • There is no way to see historical/resolved alerts beyond the three status tabs — no date filter, no search, no pagination (the API caps at 50 by default, 200 max, and the UI never passes a limit). Fine at zero events; would matter at a thousand.
  • The Dismissed tab's empty state renders as "No Dismissed alerts." with the tab name interpolated — grammatically odd on a projector. Leave the New tab selected.
  • One stubbed thumbnail (frame from video 56) failed to load in my run while others succeeded, most likely a 429 on the static path. If a card ever shows a blank frame on stage, it is a transient fetch, not a missing thumbnail.

City Map (Ahmedabad GIS + predictive forecast + patrol routing)

An Ahmedabad crime-intelligence map with three switchable data layers, a glass-box recency-weighted risk forecast whose every point is decomposed back to its source category, a rolling-origin backtest panel with baselines and an oracle ceiling, and a 2-opt patrol router that turns the forecast into physical routes from Cyber Crime Branch HQ.

Get there: Log in as admin@city.gov / admin123, then click "City Map" in the top nav (4th item). Allow ~10s for the first render — it fires four API calls plus the OSM tile layer.

Show this

  1. Click any circle on the Risk forecast layer and the popup does not just give a score — it decomposes it. Bapunagar reads "Risk 100 = prior 4 + 48 from recent assault (+2 more) over 215 reports (recency-decayed)" with a stacked bar whose segments sum exactly to the displayed score.
    Do: Layer toggle -> "Risk forecast". Click the biggest red circle (Bapunagar, north-east of centre). Read the "Why this hotspot?" line out loud. Then click the ⓘ next to "Top predicted hotspots" in the right panel to show the model card with every published coefficient.
    This is the single strongest answer to "predictive policing is a black box". Every point of the 0-100 score is attributable to a named term — the static prior, each crime category's recency-decayed contribution, and the live anomaly boost. The normalisation is affine and the -min offset is folded into the prior specifically so the bar adds up. An officer can audit why a locality got flagged; a judge can too.
  2. The "Model accuracy (backtested)" panel — real out-of-sample numbers, four ranking strategies side by side, and an oracle ceiling that makes the headline honest.
    Do: Right-hand panel, top card. Click "Model accuracy (backtested)" to expand. It is already cached server-side (~10 min TTL) so it opens instantly — I measured the API at 0.25s warm.
    Hit-Rate@10 0.78, PAI@10 2.35x against a perfect-hindsight ceiling of 2.53x = 93% of what is achievable on this data, with a 90% bootstrap CI of [0.76, 0.81] over 8 weekly rolling-origin folds. Under it, the baseline table: model 2.35x, frequency 2.22x, prior-only 1.95x, random 1.05x. Almost nobody at a hackathon puts a chance floor and a ceiling next to their own number. And the panel prints its own disclaimer that the data is synthetic.
  3. "Caught 3/3 planted crime waves" — surge detection. "Maninagar surfaced rank 5→3 the week its chain snatching wave hit."
    Do: It is the first, green-highlighted block inside the expanded accuracy panel. Read the Maninagar line verbatim.
    This is the cleanest evidence the model reacts to emerging hotspots instead of parroting a static prior. Time-boxed surges were planted in the seed; the harness scores the model at the surge's end and against the counterfactual just before it began, and reports the rank jump. Maninagar 5→3, SG Highway 6→4, Satellite 10→9, all three inside the top-10 while their surge was live.
  4. Patrol routing: press one button and the forecast becomes physical routes. 2 units, 12 hotspots, dashed polylines drawn from Cyber Crime Branch HQ (Shahibaug) — Unit 1 28.8 km / ~69 min, Unit 2 33.3 km / ~80 min.
    Do: Set the unit dropdown (top-right) to "2 units", click "Plan patrol routes". Two dashed coloured polylines appear over the map and a "Patrol plan" card lists each unit's ordered stops with km and ETA. Then click "Export CSV" on that card.
    It closes the loop from analytics to action, and the routing is real: balanced greedy assignment by centroid proximity with a ceil(n/units) fairness cap, then nearest-neighbour seeding, then 2-opt polish, all on haversine distances. Pure stdlib — no Google Directions, no external routing API, so it runs offline. Measured latency 0.05 seconds.
  5. The Cyber fraud layer with a deliberate, defensible framing: "₹2.1 Cr lost citywide · 164 victims · last 90 days" and the subtitle "Victim-location density — where victims live, not where fraudsters operate."
    Do: Layer toggle -> "Cyber fraud". The violet chip with the ₹ figure sits next to the window selector; the right panel switches to "Top fraud channels".
    For a Cyber Crime Branch this is the layer that matters, and the subtitle is the kind of methodological honesty that wins credibility — it names the limitation of the data before anyone else can. Circles are log-scaled by rupees lost, in a distinct violet palette so it never reads as the amber/red crime-density map.

If they ask

What exactly does the forecast predict, and for when?
One 7-day window ahead, per locality, on the same 0-100 scale as the current score. predicted_score = current_score × min(growth, 2.0), capped at 100, where growth = (load in the last 7 days + 1) / (load in the 7 days before that + 1). The +1 smoothing stops empty windows exploding and the ×2 cap stops one quiet fortnight producing a nonsense number. Concretely on screen right now: SG Highway 64.9 → 87.0 rising, Bapunagar 100 → 34.9 falling, Vatva 90.8 → 84.5 stable. Across all 30 areas: 9 rising, 9 stable, 12 falling.
How do you know it predicts anything? Show me the validation.
Rolling-origin (walk-forward) temporal cross-validation, 8 weekly folds over 30 localities, first fold starting 2026-06-24 and the last 2026-08-12. For each fold we score the model with an as_of cutoff so it sees ONLY complaints strictly before the fold start, then grade it against what actually happened in the following 7 days. No future data ever reaches a prediction. Current live numbers: Hit-Rate@10 0.7818, PAI@10 2.345x, Hit-Rate@5 0.5382, PAI@5 3.229x.
What is PAI and why should I care about 2.35x?
Predictive Accuracy Index, the field standard from Chainey, Tompson & Uhlig (2008): hit-rate divided by the share of the area you are policing. PAI@10 = (crime captured in the top 10 areas / total crime) ÷ (10/30). PAI = 1 is exactly chance — you get 33% of the crime by covering 33% of the city. 2.345 means we concentrate 2.3x more crime per unit of patrol area than blind coverage. Our random baseline measured 1.053x, which is the sanity check that the metric is wired up correctly.
2.35x doesn't sound like much. Is that good?
That is the right question, and it is why we publish the oracle ceiling. If you rank each fold by its OWN realised next-week counts — perfect hindsight, the best any predictor could possibly do — PAI@10 is only 2.529x on this data. So 2.345 is 93% of the theoretical maximum. The ceiling is low because the synthetic stream is fairly stationary and lightly contrasted; on lumpier real data both numbers would rise. The honest claim is the percentage of the ceiling, not the absolute.
What are the confidence intervals and how were they computed?
90% intervals by bootstrapping over folds: resample the 8 per-fold metrics with replacement 1,000 times, take the 5th and 95th percentiles of the resampled means, fixed seed 20240601 so it reproduces exactly. Hit-Rate@10 [0.757, 0.809]; PAI@10 [2.271, 2.428]; Hit-Rate@5 [0.499, 0.577]. Pure stdlib random/math — no numpy, no sklearn, so it runs anywhere the backend does.
What do you compare against? Isn't a naive count just as good?
That is nearly true and we publish it rather than hide it. Four strategies on identical folds, PAI@10: model 2.345x, raw all-time frequency 2.220x, static prior-only 1.953x, random 1.053x. So the full model beats a naive complaint count by about 5%. Worse — and this is in our ablation doc — the stripped-down floor actually BEATS the full model at k=5 (Hit-Rate 0.552 vs 0.540). Our advantage exists at k=10 and vanishes at k=5. The headline HR@10 hides that, and I would rather say it than have you find it.
Your model has a prior, severity weights, category weights, recency decay, an anomaly boost and a trend term. Which of those actually do anything?
Only recency decay, and we measured it. A component ablation (backend/scripts/ablation.py --folds 8, which regenerates docs/ABLATION.md so the doc cannot drift from the code) gives paired ΔHit-Rate@10 with bootstrap CIs: removing recency decay costs -0.045 [-0.068, -0.027] — the only statistically distinguishable term. Static prior +0.012 [0.000, +0.026] and category weighting +0.006 [-0.015, +0.027] point the WRONG way (removing them slightly raised HR@10) but do not clear significance, so we quote them as a direction, never as proven harm. Severity weighting -0.005 [-0.020, +0.005], indistinguishable.
And the anomaly boost? It's in your formula and on your model card.
It contributes EXACTLY ZERO to the backtest — 0.000, by construction, not by measurement noise. compute_risk() applies the anomaly boost only when as_of is None, and every validation fold passes an as_of, so the term is never evaluated during scoring. That exclusion is deliberate and correct: anomaly events reflect NOW and have no historical timeline, so feeding present-time anomalies into a historical fold would be straight temporal leakage. The honest framing is 'deliberately excluded to avoid leakage', not 'it improves accuracy'. Same for the trend term: TREND_WINDOW_DAYS shapes predicted_score, but compute_risk ranks folds on risk_score, so trend is 0.000 in the backtest too. The README's criteria table was corrected specifically for this.
So if the anomaly boost does nothing, why is it in the model at all?
Because the backtest and the live map are answering different questions. The backtest grades a 7-day-ahead forecast from historical complaints. The live map is an operational picture where an active fire or weapon detection on an area-tagged camera SHOULD push that locality up the board right now — that is a nowcast, not a forecast, and there is no way to backtest it because we have no historical anomaly stream. It is currently contributing 0 on screen anyway: active_anomalies is 0 for all 30 areas because no anomaly events exist and the 16 seeded videos have no area tag.
Isn't this just fitting your own synthetic data generator?
Partly yes, and that is the biggest caveat. 'Recency decay wins' is close to a test of how seed_synthetic.py generates data. What the backtest genuinely demonstrates is the METHODOLOGY — no-leakage rolling-origin folds, standard hotspot metrics, published baselines, a ceiling, and CIs — not real-world operational accuracy. The API returns that disclaimer as a field and the UI renders it verbatim at the bottom of the accuracy panel. We also have a self-check that proves the harness is real: flatten every weight and the model must collapse to a raw complaint count and exactly reproduce validation.py's independently-written frequency baseline. It does — 0.7420 == 0.7420.
Eight folds is a small sample.
Agreed, and it limits what we can claim. 'Not distinguishable' in our ablation is partly a statement about power, not about the component being useless. The fold count is capped at 26 in the API (?folds=N) if you want to trade window length for more folds. We also quote paired deltas rather than absolute levels, because levels drift as the 180-day synthetic window slides but the paired deltas are stable.
How were the surges detected, and could you have planted them to be easy?
They are planted — that is the point, it gives us ground truth — but the detection is fair. For each planted surge we score the model at the surge's END (so it has seen the surge build) and separately at the day BEFORE it began, and report the rank change. Maninagar 5→3, SG Highway 6→4, Satellite 10→9; all three in the top 10 during their surge week, 2/2 surges caught. We report per-SURGE, not per-area, because Satellite sits exactly on the top-10 boundary and lands on either side of it between runs as the 180-day window slides. Per-surge reproduces; per-area does not, and a headline that changes between runs is worse than useless.
Show me the model. What are the actual coefficients?
Click the ⓘ — the model card publishes all of them, and the API returns them in the same payload as the scores so nothing can drift. risk = prior + Σ w_severity · w_category · 0.5^(age/14 days) + 1.5 · active_anomalies, min-max scaled to 0-100. Severity: low 1, medium 2, high 3, critical 4. Category: murder ×2.0, body_offences ×1.6, assault ×1.5, chain_snatching ×1.3, burglary/narcotics ×1.2, vehicle_theft ×1.1, theft/cyber_fraud/prohibition ×1.0. Prior intensity: low 1, medium 3, high 6, from public NCRB/police/press reporting so a fresh deployment ranks sensibly before any live data arrives. Trend window 7 days. Bands: high ≥75, elevated ≥50, guarded ≥25, low below.
Predictive policing has a well-known bias problem — you patrol where you predicted, generate more reports there, and the model confirms itself.
Correct, and we do not claim to have solved it. Three things we do do: (1) the model is fed by CITIZEN complaints, not officer stops or arrests, which is the weaker of the two feedback loops but still a loop; (2) every score is decomposed on screen, so a supervisor can see a locality is ranked high because of a static prior rather than recent reports and discount it; (3) the static prior is published as a table with three coarse levels rather than hidden in learned weights. What we do NOT have is a drift monitor or a patrol-log feedback correction — patrol_logs are captured but are not fed back into the score. That is honest future work, not a shipped feature.
How does the patrol router work? Is that a real optimiser or a straight line?
Real, and pure stdlib so it works offline. Take the top-12 hotspots from the live model; assign them to units with a balanced greedy pass — walk hotspots by risk rank and give each to the unit whose running centroid is nearest, with a hard ceil(n/units) cap so one unit cannot hoard stops; then order each unit's stops with nearest-neighbour from the HQ depot and polish with 2-opt until no segment reversal shortens the tour. Distances are haversine. For a ~30-node city graph 2-opt is close enough to exact and needs no external routing API. Measured 0.05s for a 2-unit plan.
Those routes are straight lines, not roads.
Yes — deliberately. They are haversine great-circle legs between locality centroids, not turn-by-turn navigation. Adding OSRM or Google Directions would make the polylines follow roads and the km/ETA more accurate, but it would also make the whole feature require internet, which is the opposite of the deployment constraint. The ETA already bakes in a 25 km/h city patrol pace including observation stops, so the ~69 min for 28.8 km is an operational estimate, not a driving time.
Does the patrol plan respect the category and time filters I set on the map?
No, and that is a genuine limitation. patrol_routes() calls compute_risk() with no filters — it always plans over the all-time top-12 hotspots regardless of what the Reports layer is filtered to. If you filter to Chain snatching and then plan routes, you get the general crime routes, not chain-snatching routes.
Why doesn't the 7/30/90-day selector change the risk forecast?
Because the risk model has its own time weighting built in — a 14-day exponential half-life — so a hard window cut-off would double-count the recency handling and throw away the long tail the prior relies on. The UI reflects that: switching to the Risk forecast layer hides the category and window selectors entirely rather than leaving dead controls on screen.
Your README says Hit-Rate@10 0.790 and PAI@10 2.37x but the screen says 0.78 and 2.35x.
The synthetic window is 180 days ending at seed time, so as it slides the weekend uplift falls on different area/category/day combinations and the totals drift a little run to run (~1,970-2,001 complaints). The README documents that explicitly. Always read the number off the screen, never off the deck.
Where does the Ahmedabad locality data come from?
30 named localities with real centroids in backend/app/constants/ahmedabad.py, and a per-area crime-intensity profile compiled from public NCRB / police / press reporting in seed_ahmedabad.py. The complaint stream itself is synthetic and disclosed as such on this very screen. The patrol depot is the real Cyber Crime Branch at Shahibaug (23.053, 72.593).
What is that '10 linked cases' in the header, and why does Vatva's popup say Cases: 0?
The header count is citywide cases that have an area tag; the per-area popup counts cases linked to that specific locality. Vatva has 263 complaints but no case has been opened against it yet — the 10 cases are concentrated elsewhere. It is consistent, but it does look odd side by side and I would not click Vatva while the header says 10.

Careful / known limits

  • NO LEGEND. There is no colour key anywhere on the map. The bands (low / guarded / elevated / high, and the amber→orange→red density ramp) only appear inside tooltips, popups and the side-panel score chips. If a judge points at an orange circle and asks what orange means, the presenter has to answer verbally. This is the single most visible gap on an otherwise excellent screen.
  • MAP CAN HANG ON "Loading city intelligence…" INDEFINITELY. Observed twice, past 45 seconds each time, while the shared API rate-limit bucket was exhausted. The render is gated on BOTH /analytics/map and /predict/risk having returned, and refresh() swallows every failure with .catch(()=>{}) — so there is no error message, no retry button, and nothing tells the presenter what happened. It only recovers on the next 30s poll. If this happens on stage, the fix is: wait 30 seconds, or switch to another module and back.
  • RATE LIMIT IS THE REAL STAGE RISK. 120 requests/minute, keyed on request.client.host — and because nginx proxies /api, the backend sees the nginx container IP for EVERY browser, so all clients share ONE bucket. One idle City Map tab burns 74 req/min (measured: health 20, videos 20, anomalies/summary 12, notifications 12, plus the four map endpoints twice each). Two tabs open ≈ 148/min and everything starts 429-ing. On stage: exactly one browser tab, and make sure no second machine has the app open.
  • NO FORECAST HORIZON OR DATE CONTROL. The forecast is fixed at one 7-day window ahead and the backtest is hard-coded to folds=8, compare=true. Do not offer to "change the lookahead" — there is no control for it. The API does accept ?folds=N up to 26 if a judge insists on seeing it move.
  • Patrol planner ignores the map's category and time filters — it always plans over the all-time top-12 hotspots. Don't set a category filter and then say "and here are the routes for THAT crime type".
  • Route polyline tooltips did not fire for me on hover — the dashed line is only 3px wide. Quote the km/ETA off the side-panel Patrol plan card instead of trying to hover a line on stage.
  • Bapunagar is the biggest, reddest circle (risk 100) but its forecast is 34.9 FALLING. If you click it while talking about prediction, the story deflates. Click Bapunagar for the score DECOMPOSITION, and SG Highway (64.9 → 87.0, rising) for the FORECAST.
  • The Reports popup for Vatva shows "Cases: 0" while the page header says "10 linked cases". Both are correct (citywide vs per-area) but it reads as an inconsistency. Prefer clicking Maninagar or Bapunagar if a judge is reading the numbers closely.
  • Filtering to a sparse category (Prohibition = 1 complaint, Flood / disaster = 2) leaves the map almost entirely flat dim-steel with all 30 circles at minimum radius. Nothing errors — it just has nothing to show.
  • The README quotes HR@10 0.790 / PAI@10 2.37x; the live API returns 0.78 / 2.35x because the 180-day synthetic window slides. Read numbers off the screen, not the deck.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 30 Ahmedabad localities scored, 2,001 reports used, recomputed live on every request
  • Top-5 risk: Bapunagar 100.0 (high), Naroda 98.5 (high), Vatva 90.8 (high), SG Highway 64.9-66 (elevated), Maninagar 58.2-58.3 (elevated)

  • Bapunagar score decomposition: prior 4.07 + assault 48.08 + body_offences 38.19 + theft 9.66 + anomaly 0.00 = 100.0

  • Hit-Rate@10 0.7818, 90% CI [0.757, 0.809] (1,000 bootstrap resamples over folds, seed 20240601)
  • Hit-Rate@5 0.5382, 90% CI [0.499, 0.577]
  • PAI@10 2.345x, 90% CI [2.271, 2.428]; PAI@5 3.229x, 90% CI [2.993, 3.465]

  • Ablation paired ΔHR@10: recency decay -0.045 [-0.068, -0.027] (the ONLY significant term); prior +0.012 [0.000, +0.026]; category weighting +0.006 [-0.015, +0.027]; severity -0.005 [-0.020, +0.005]; anomaly boost 0.000 exact; trend 0.000 exact
  • Ablation self-check: flattening all weights reproduces the independent frequency baseline exactly, 0.7420 == 0.7420

Arbiter — Legal Intelligence (Section Lookup · FIR Draft · Legal Q&A)

An offline RAG legal assistant over a 25-section IPC / IT Act 2000 / CrPC corpus that ranks applicable sections, drafts a registration-format FIR, and answers plain-language legal questions — no internet and no API key involved.

Get there: Sign in at http://localhost:8080 as admin@city.gov / admin123 → top nav, 5th tab "Arbiter". The three tabs are mode buttons inside the panel: Section Lookup (default) / FIR Draft / Legal Q&A. Officer role also sees this tab.

Show this

  1. Type a plain-language incident and get ranked statutes with punishments back in ~70 ms, fully offline. "Caller impersonating a bank official obtained the OTP and transferred Rs 2,40,000" returns IT Act 66C Identity theft at 52%, 66D Cheating by personation at 37%, 72 Breach of confidentiality at 35% — exactly the three sections an Ahmedabad cyber cell would file.
    Do: Arbiter tab → Section Lookup (already selected) → paste the query into the textarea → click "Find Sections". Result renders in under a second.
    It is semantic retrieval, not keyword grep: the words "identity theft", "personation" and "confidentiality" never appear in the query. And the amber badge in the corner says "Offline mode" — this runs on a laptop in a police station with the network unplugged.
  2. FIR Draft: one narrative paragraph plus four fields produces a complete registration-format FIR with numbered BRIEF FACTS / APPLICABLE LEGAL PROVISIONS / PRAYER sections, complainant and accused filled in, signature and P.S. lines, plus a provenance badge reading "Template draft" and an amber disclaimer.
    Do: Click "FIR Draft" → paste the incident into the textarea → fill the four inputs (Complainant / Accused / Place / Date-time) → click "Draft FIR".
    It is a document an officer could hand across the counter, generated in 71 ms, and it is explicitly labelled as machine-drafted rather than pretending to be a filed FIR. The section chips above the draft are the same retrieval that powers Section Lookup, so the citations are grounded in a real corpus and cannot be hallucinated.
  3. Mode switching preserves the textarea, so the same facts flow through all three tabs without retyping: describe the incident in Section Lookup, click FIR Draft, click Draft FIR.
    It is the actual officer workflow in three clicks, and it lets you show all three capabilities on one set of facts inside 30 seconds of stage time.
  4. Legal Q&A answers a citizen's question with a cited, plain-language answer plus the full section cards underneath and citation chips.
    Do: Click "Legal Q&A" → type the question → click "Get Answer".
    Same corpus, different audience — the panel doubles as a public-facing legal helpdesk, which is the "citizen portal" half of CityShield's story.
  5. The "Offline mode · 25 sections" badge itself, explained.
    Judges expect an LLM wrapper. This is ChromaDB with the built-in MiniLM ONNX embedder, persisted locally, so retrieval is real vector search with zero external calls; Gemini is an optional polish layer that is currently off (llm_online=false) and the system degrades to a deterministic, citation-grounded template rather than breaking.

If they ask

That badge says "Offline mode". So there is no AI here — it is a lookup table?
No. Retrieval is real semantic search: the 25 sections are embedded once into a persistent ChromaDB collection with Chroma's built-in MiniLM ONNX embedder and queried by cosine similarity. The proof is that "Caller impersonating a bank official obtained the OTP" returns IT Act 66C "Identity theft" at 52% when the words identity and theft never appear in the query. What is offline is the *generation* step — with no Gemini key set (llm_online=false) the prose is composed from a deterministic template around the retrieved sections, rather than written by an LLM.
What changes if you plug in a Gemini key?
Three things, all visible in the UI. The badge flips to green "Gemini online"; the FIR provenance chip flips from "Template draft" to "Gemini draft"; and the language dropdown starts working, because translation is delegated to the same LLM bridge. Retrieval does not change at all — Gemini is only allowed to compose prose around sections that retrieval already found, and there is an output validator that rejects the generation and falls back to the template if the model cites a section that was not in the retrieved set. That is a deliberate anti-hallucination guard, not an accident of being offline.
How do I know it will not invent a section number?
Structurally it cannot. `suggest_sections` returns rows straight out of the vector store — every citation is a row in the corpus JSON. For the LLM path, `guards.validate_output` checks the generated text against the set of citations that were actually retrieved and discards the generation if it cites anything else. The corpus is 25 hand-written entries in backend/app/arbiter/corpus/legal_corpus.json, so the failure mode is silence, not fiction.
Only 25 sections? The IPC alone has 511.
Correct, and that is the honest ceiling. The 25 are a curated cyber-crime-first set — 10 IT Act 2000 sections, 12 IPC, 3 CrPC — chosen for the Ahmedabad Cyber Crime Branch's actual caseload. It has real gaps: no 498A, no 304B dowry death, no 363/364A kidnapping, no 376, no NI Act 138, and no BNS 2023 at all. Ask it about kidnapping for ransom and it confidently returns IPC 392 Robbery. Widening it is a data task not an engineering task — the ingestion is `coll.upsert` over a JSON file and re-indexing is idempotent on restart. Notably CrimeGPT's Suggest Sections tab already carries a separate BNS 2023 / BNSS catalogue, so the two together cover both statutory eras.
Why does chain snatching return "IT Act 66C Identity theft" and "IPC 354D Stalking"?
Because retrieval returns exactly top-k with no relevance floor — it always hands back 6 rows even when only two are above 30% similarity. The match percentage on each card is the honest signal and the bad hits sit at 17-21%. The right product fix is a score cutoff (drop anything under ~0.30) which would have shown two sections instead of six here. It is one line in `store.retrieve`; it is not in yet.
Does it tell me whether the offence is cognizable and bailable? That is the first thing a duty officer needs.
The data is there and the API returns it — POST /api/legal/sections gives cognizable and bailable per section — but the UI card does not render either field today. It shows citation, match %, title, summary and punishment only. That is a genuine gap between the backend and the front end, not something we are hiding.
You offer Hindi and Gujarati in the dropdown. Show me a Hindi answer.
It will come back in English, and I would rather say that than click it. Translation runs through the same LLM bridge as generation, and with no key configured the bridge returns None and the template falls through in English. The interface chrome is fully translated in all three languages — 706 keys — but the generated legal text is not. Do not demo that dropdown.
Is this legally safe? What if an officer files it as-is?
Two layers. Every FIR carries a "Template draft" or "Gemini draft" provenance chip so nobody can mistake which produced it, plus an amber disclaimer and an in-document NOTE requiring the IO to verify facts and section applicability before registration. And Arbiter never writes to a case — it produces a draft in the browser. The section suggestion is advisory; registration remains a human act.
How fast is it, and what does it cost to run?
Measured server-side over 5 runs each: section lookup 69 ms median, FIR draft 71 ms, Q&A 60 ms. Cost is zero — no API calls, no GPU. The whole legal brain is a ChromaDB directory on disk.
Prompt injection — the incident text is user-supplied. What stops "ignore your instructions"?
Every user string goes through `guards.sanitize_user_text` and is then wrapped in explicit <<<UNTRUSTED_…>>> fences before it reaches the model, and the system prompt states that fenced content is data and never an instruction. On top of that the output validator rejects any generation citing sections outside the retrieved set. Offline today none of it is exercised because there is no model — but the guard is in the code path, not bolted on later.

Careful / known limits

  • cognizable and bailable never render in the UI. The API returns them per section; ArbiterPanel's SectionCard draws only citation / match% / title / summary / punishment. Do not promise a judge the bailability flag on this screen.
  • The language dropdown on FIR Draft and Legal Q&A is dead offline. Selecting हिन्दी or ગુજરાતી returns byte-identical English (measured). Presenter should not click it.
  • No relevance floor — retrieval always returns exactly top_k. "Two men snatched a gold chain" returns IT Act 66C Identity theft and IPC 354D Stalking at 17-21% alongside the correct IPC 379/392.
  • DO NOT DEMO: kidnapping / ransom. "Ransom demand after kidnapping a minor boy from school" returns IPC 392 Robbery, IPC 379 Theft, CrPC 41. There is no kidnapping or abduction section in the 25-item corpus.
  • DO NOT DEMO: dowry / matrimonial cruelty. "Man assaulted his wife demanding dowry" returns IPC 323, 506, 307 and then IT Act 67B (child sexually explicit material) at 22%. There is no 498A or 304B in the corpus and the 67B hit looks terrible on a projector.
  • DO NOT DEMO: "Someone is sending me threatening messages on WhatsApp every day" in Legal Q&A — it cites IT Act 66F Cyber terrorism (punishment up to life imprisonment) as the second-most-relevant provision.
  • Legal Q&A does not actually answer the question asked; it lists the three most relevant sections with their punishments and a closing line. Asking "is this bailable?" gets you sections but never a yes or no. Frame it as "cited legal grounding", not "a chatbot".
  • The FIR draft is stamped with the server clock, which runs UTC inside the container. At 00:52 IST on 20 Aug the draft read "Date of report : 2026-08-19 19:22". Harmless for a morning demo (10:00 IST = 04:30 UTC, same date) but the date will look a day behind if anyone demos late at night.
  • Nothing crashed. Zero pageerrors and zero /api/ responses ≥400 across every Arbiter interaction tested.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • Corpus: 25 sections — 10 IT Act 2000, 12 IPC, 3 CrPC (counted from legal_corpus.json and confirmed by GET /api/legal/health corpus_sections=25)
  • Section lookup latency: 69 ms median server-side (5 runs; min 47 ms, max 71 ms). First call after a cold start took 0.87 s end-to-end in the browser, subsequent ~0.15 s.
  • FIR draft latency: 71 ms median server-side (5 runs; min 61, max 84)
  • Legal Q&A latency: 60 ms median server-side (5 runs; min 44, max 71)
  • Best measured retrieval score: IT Act 66C at 52% for the OTP-fraud query; IT Act 66 at 45% for the server-intrusion query
  • Weakest measured retrieval: 17% top score for "stalking"-adjacent dowry query — i.e. the corpus had nothing relevant and still returned 6 rows
  • llm_online = false, mode = "offline (RAG + templates)", languages advertised = en/hi/gu (only en produces output)
  • 0 JavaScript pageerrors, 0 API responses ≥400 across all Arbiter testing

CrimeGPT — case-document generator

Enter the case facts once into a unified data pool, then generate all seven statutory Indian-police documents as branded, versioned PDFs — each one drawing the slice of the pool it actually needs.

Get there: Sign in as admin@city.gov / admin123 → top nav, 6th tab "CrimeGPT". Case picker is top-right (4 open cases). Four tabs: Data Pool / Suggest Sections / Documents / Case Diary. Data Pool has three sub-tabs: Parties / Seizures / Statements. Use case "#29 · Digital-arrest cyber-fraud ring busted" — it is the only one with a populated pool.

Show this

  1. Seven statutory documents from one data pool, in seven clicks. I generated every one of Purvani Chargesheet, Medical Treatment Letter, Remand Request (BNSS s.187), Seizure Receipt, Court Custody Letter, Accused Panchanama and Face Identification Form — all seven downloaded, all seven opened cleanly, 51 ms median server-side.
    Do: CrimeGPT tab → case #29 → "Documents" tab → click "Generate PDF" on each card. Each fires a browser download named e.g. accused_panchanama_case29_v1_en.pdf.
    This is the paperwork that eats an investigating officer's week. The officer types the accused, the victim, the muddamal and the statements exactly once and the whole statutory file falls out, correctly formatted, on Cyber Crime Branch letterhead, with FIR number and police station in the header of every page.
  2. The documents are genuinely different, not one template with a swapped title. The Panchanama pulls the seizures in as "Articles Found on the Accused" and prints a physical-description line per accused; the Medical Letter names only the single person to be examined and asks for an MLC plus FSL sample preservation; the Face Identification Form emits a photograph box per accused with Complexion/Build/Height/Face-shape blanks and a "VisionScan facial-match reference" field; the Seizure Receipt is a 5-column panchnama with date, place and both panch witnesses per item.
    That last one is the platform tie-in a judge will notice: the face-ID form has a slot for the similarity score and camera timestamp coming out of VisionScan, so the AI evidence lands in the statutory record rather than in a screenshot.
  3. Every generation is auto-written to the Case Diary (Roznamcha) with version, officer name and timestamp — case #29 carries 18 entries covering parties added, seizures recorded, statements taken and each document version generated.
    It is an audit trail nobody has to remember to write. Adding an accused writes "Accused 'Rakesh Bhanushali' added to the case file"; generating a PDF writes "Purvani Chargesheet v2 generated by Station Admin". Chain of custody by construction.
  4. Suggest Sections works off BNS 2023 with the pre-2023 IPC cross-reference printed next to it, plus BNSS procedure and the exact terms that matched.
    Do: "Suggest Sections" tab → paste the narrative in the box → click Suggest. Use the exact narrative in the inputs section — the built-in case description only scores 2.
    India switched criminal codes on 1 July 2024 and most officers still think in IPC. Showing "BNS 318 — Cheating (IPC 415/420)" side by side is the bridge, and printing the matched terms means the officer can see why it fired instead of trusting a black box.
  5. The two fixes shipped today, both verified in a generated PDF: the language line now reads "Language: EN (requested HI — translation needs an LLM key)" instead of falsely claiming HI, and the Station Seal now has a real 4.6 cm × 2.6 cm bordered box underneath the label.
    The first is an integrity fix on an evidentiary record — the document states what it actually is. The second is the kind of physical-world detail that tells a police audience someone thought about the rubber stamp.

If a judge asks, generate the HI one deliberately and read that line out — it is a strength, not a bug.
Data Pool — if you want to add a party live on stageRole: Accused · Name: Rakesh Bhanushali · Age: 31 · Gender: Male · Address: B-402 Sunrise Apartments, Naroda, Ahmedabad · Phone: 9825011223 · ID: Aadhaar XXXX-XXXX-4417 · Description: Operated the fake police video-call setupAppears instantly in the list with a red ACCUSED badge, writes a diary entry, and shows up in the next generated PDF. Use Latin script only — Devanagari names render with misplaced vowel signs (see limitations).

If they ask

Can it produce a Hindi or Gujarati document? The interface is trilingual.
The interface is, the PDF body is not, and I want to be exact about why. Two separate things are missing. First, translation: the narrative goes through the Arbiter LLM bridge, which is offline with no Gemini key, so the English text passes through unchanged — and as of today the PDF states this on its face rather than lying, it prints "Language: EN (requested HI — translation needs an LLM key)". Second, and this one no API key fixes: ReportLab does no Indic text shaping. We bundle Noto Sans Devanagari and Gujarati and they embed correctly — you can put a Hindi name in the pool and it appears in the PDF — but ReportLab lays glyphs out in logical order, and Devanagari requires reordering. The short-i vowel sign ि is stored after its consonant and must be drawn before it. So चेन स्नैचिंग comes out as चेन स्नैचगि, डिजिटल as डजिटिल, त्वरित as त्वरति. Base letters and most matras are fine; anything with ि is visibly wrong. The correct fix is a shaping engine — HarfBuzz via uharfbuzz, or rendering through WeasyPrint/Pango instead of ReportLab — which is a real piece of work, not a config flag. So today: use English for the document body. The UI, the case data and the officer's workflow are trilingual; the printed evidentiary record is English.
So a judge should not ask you to print a Hindi chargesheet.
They can, and I would show it — it will print an English body with an accurate label saying that is what happened. What I will not do is put Hindi paragraphs in an evidentiary document and let broken conjuncts go into a court file. Stamping "Language: HI" on an English document was a false statement on a record and we fixed that this morning; printing garbled Devanagari would be the same mistake wearing a different hat.
These look like Word templates with mail-merge. What is the AI part?
The document assembly is deterministic by design — you do not want a language model inventing the wording of a remand application. Two AI pieces sit around it. The section suggester scores the narrative against a curated BNS 2023 / BNSS / BSA catalogue and prints the terms that matched, so the officer sees the reasoning. And the pool itself is fed by the rest of the platform — the Face Identification Form has a slot for VisionScan's facial-match reference, similarity score and camera timestamp. The value here is not generative prose, it is that seven statutory documents share one source of truth and stay consistent with each other.
How do you know the BNS section numbers are right? Wrong sections in front of police judges are fatal.
They were cross-checked against the enacted BNS 2023, BNSS 2023 and BSA 2023 texts, and the source file says so at the top with a standing warning. Where a mapping is broad the entry carries verify=True and the UI surfaces a "verify before use" caution. Landmark judgments are attached only where squarely on point — otherwise the list is left empty rather than risk a bad cite. And the IPC cross-references are labelled as the closest pre-2023 equivalent for officer familiarity, not a claim of identical scope. Every card also carries "Always verify the exact clause against the bare act before filing."
Why did my narrative return zero sections?
Because the suggester is a deterministic substring matcher over 30 offence patterns, not a semantic model — deliberately, so it is auditable and can show you exactly which words fired. The cost is that it misses paraphrase. I measured it: "posing as a CBI officer… digital arrest… mule accounts" scores zero, because none of cheating/fraud/otp/phishing/personation/fake/scam appear. Add "online fraud" and "OTP" to the same facts and it scores 10 across three patterns. That is a real gap, and the fix is the same ChromaDB retrieval Arbiter already uses — the infrastructure is already in the repo, it just is not wired to this catalogue yet.
Where do the generated PDFs live? Can they be tampered with?
Each generation is recorded server-side with case, doc type, language, an incrementing version, the generating officer's id and a timestamp, and it writes an immutable Case Diary entry. Versions never overwrite — the chargesheet on case 29 is at v9 because I regenerated it during load testing, and all nine are in the history. What is not there yet is a content hash or signature on the stored bytes, so this is tamper-evident at the metadata level, not cryptographically tamper-proof.
How fast, and can it do a whole station's caseload?
51 ms median server-side for the 2-page chargesheet, measured over 5 runs; the single-page documents are faster. The whole file of seven documents for one case is well under a second of compute. Generation is pure CPU with no external calls, so throughput is bounded by the box, not by an API quota.
What happens if generation fails?
Right now, nothing visible — the grid catches the error and drops it, so the button stops spinning and no file arrives. That is the weakest thing on this screen and it is a two-line fix. The backend does the right thing (returns a 500 with the reason); the front end just does not show it.

Careful / known limits

  • Version History rows and Case Diary entries STILL claim "HI" / "GU" for documents whose body is English. The fix landed in the PDF header only. Source: backend/app/crimegpt/routes.py:199 writes f"({language.upper()})" into the diary narrative, and svc.record_document stores the requested language, which the history row renders. Same false claim, two surfaces.
  • Indic PDF bodies cannot be rendered correctly. Verified in a real generated PDF: चेन स्नैचिंग → स्नैचगि, डिजिटल → डजिटिल, त्वरित → त्वरति. Font embeds fine (AAAAAA+NotoSansDevanagari-Regular in the PDF font table); ReportLab does no shaping so the short-i vowel sign is drawn on the wrong side of its consonant. Do not type Devanagari or Gujarati into the pool, and do not print an Indic document on stage.
  • Suggest Sections returns ZERO matches for a realistic digital-arrest narrative — the flagship case's own subject matter. It is a substring matcher over 30 patterns; use the tuned narrative in the inputs list.
  • Suggest Sections mis-fires on "obscene": a narrative about obscene photographs published online matches "Obscene acts / songs in public" (BNS 296 / IPC 294, which is public obscenity), not IT Act 67. Do not run that narrative.
  • Failed generations are silent. DocumentGrid catches the exception into an empty block — no toast, no error, the button just stops spinning.
  • If GET /api/cases fails or is rate-limited, CrimeGPT silently renders the "no open cases" empty state with no error. Reproduced under 429. It looks like the platform has no cases at all.
  • Cases #30, #34 and #35 have completely empty data pools. Generating from them produces documents full of "Nil" and empty tables. Only case #29 is demo-ready — I populated it during testing (4 parties, 2 seizures, 2 statements).
  • Purvani Chargesheet on case #29 now reads v9 because of my latency benchmarking. It is correct versioning, but if a judge asks why version 9, the answer is load testing, not nine revisions of the case.
  • Cosmetic: the Station Seal box is specified with LINESTYLE "dashed" but renders as a solid hairline — ReportLab did not apply the dash. The box itself is correct size and position.
  • Nothing crashed. Zero JavaScript pageerrors across all CrimeGPT testing.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • 7 document types generated, 7 downloaded and opened successfully
  • Page counts: Purvani Chargesheet 2 pages; the other 6 are 1 page each
  • PDF sizes: 3,560–5,266 bytes for English; 15,192 bytes when a Devanagari font subset has to be embedded
  • PDF generation latency: 51 ms median server-side for the 2-page chargesheet (5 runs; min 44 ms, max 71 ms). 0.22–1.48 s end-to-end in the browser including the download.
  • Section-suggester latency: 20 ms median (5 runs)
  • Offence patterns in the BNS catalogue: 30 (GET /api/crimegpt/health offence_patterns=30)
  • Case #29 pool after testing: 4 parties (2 accused, 1 victim, 1 witness), 2 seizures, 2 statements
  • Case #29 Case Diary: 18 entries; 15 documents generated across 3 languages (12 en, 2 gu, 1 hi)
  • Station Seal box measured from the PDF drawing operators: 130.4 × 73.7 pt = 4.60 cm × 2.60 cm
  • Devanagari shaping failure rate: every word containing the vowel sign ि rendered incorrectly (3 of 3 such words in the test string); words without it rendered correctly (राकेश, भानुशाली, नरोडा, अमदावाद, पुरुष, अरेस्ट, घोटाला)

Legal Feed (GovIntel) — unified GR / notification / judgment search

One semantic search across a bundled 33-document corpus of Acts, GRs, notifications, circulars, rules, judgments and schemes plus live government RSS, with per-document AI summaries, typed cross-links between related documents, bookmarks, saved searches and alerting.

Get there: Sign in as any role (this is the one module a citizen can also reach) → top nav, 7th tab "Legal Feed". Two sub-tabs: Search and Saved. "Refresh feeds" only appears for lead-and-above (admin sees it, officer does not).

Show this

  1. Open any document's Details drawer and you get two things static search does not have: an offline extractive AI summary that condenses the document to 3-4 bullets in 15 ms, and a Related Documents list with TYPED, SCORED links — "linked 100%", "same family 69%", "similar 73%".
    Do: Legal Feed → search "e-Zero FIR" → click "Details" on the first card → click "Summarise (en)" → then click any Related Document to traverse.
    The I4C e-Zero FIR notification returns the Gujarat e-Zero FIR notification and the 1930 helpline notification as curated 100% links, BNSS 2023 as a 100% link, then IT Act 2000 as "same family 69%" and CrPC 1973 as "similar 67%". That is a legal citation graph — you can walk from a central notification to its Gujarat implementation to the statute it operates under, and every hop is labelled with WHY it is linked. Clicking a related card opens it in the same drawer, so you can traverse live.
  2. 33 bundled documents, and every single official Source link resolves. I checked all 33 programmatically (32 returned HTTP 200 outright) and spot-checked the last one plus 9 others in a real browser — all 200 with the correct page title, including the Gujarat Police Innovation Challenge press release, the ANI report on Gujarat's e-Zero FIR launch, indiacode.nic.in's BNS 2023 PDF, and indiankanoon for Puttaswamy and the 2024 JRCA v. S. Harish judgment.
    Judges assume a demo corpus is fake. Clicking "Source" on the Gujarat Police Innovation Challenge card lands on gujaratinformation.gujarat.gov.in showing "An Important Step Towards Smart Policing by Gujarat Police". These are real government documents with live provenance.
  3. "Refresh feeds" pulls live government RSS in 1.4 seconds and is idempotent — 20 items from PIB Gujarat, and re-running reports "Fetched 20, 0 new, 0 alert(s) sent" instead of duplicating them. The header badge and the DOCUMENTS INDEXED tile move from 33 to 53.
    It is not a static dump. The corpus is the offline floor and the RSS layer is the live ceiling, and the pipeline is stdlib-only (urllib + ElementTree) so there is no dependency to break in a police network.
  4. Exact facet counts that add up: GR 2 · Notification 7 · Circular 2 · Act 8 · Rule 2 · Judgment 4 · Scheme 8 = 33. Region chips give Central 27 / Gujarat 26 after the live pull. Department dropdown has 21 real issuing authorities, from CERT-In and I4C to the Supreme Court of India and Gujarat Police Cyber Centre of Excellence.
    Clicking "Judgment" gives exactly the 4 judgments — Shreya Singhal, Puttaswamy, CBSE v. Aditya Bandopadhyay, JRCA v. S. Harish. Clicking department "Supreme Court of India" gives the same 4. The taxonomy is real, not decorative.
  5. Saved searches with alerting, bookmarks and topic subscriptions, wired into the platform's existing notification bell — and correct RBAC: an officer sees Save search but not Refresh feeds, matching the server-side require_role("lead") gate.
    It closes the loop: an officer saves "e-Zero FIR" with the Alert box ticked, and the next refresh that finds a matching new document raises a notification in the same bell that carries their case alerts. The UI does not show a button the server would 403.

If they ask

Is this a real corpus or did you type 33 fake documents?
Real, and every one is clickable. I verified all 33 source links: 32 return HTTP 200 to a script, and the 33rd plus nine others I opened in an actual browser — all 200 with the right page title. The Acts point at indiacode.nic.in PDFs, the judgments at indiankanoon, the CERT-In directions at cert-in.org.in, the Gujarat Police Innovation Challenge at gujaratinformation.gujarat.gov.in. Seven of those links were broken and were fixed today. Click any Source button you like.
The badge says 53 documents but you said the corpus is 33.
Both are true and the difference is the point. 33 is the bundled offline corpus that ships with the image and works with the network unplugged. The other 20 came in live from PIB Gujarat's RSS the last time someone clicked Refresh feeds. The header badge and the DOCUMENTS INDEXED tile show the union; the tile also breaks it out as "20 live". Unplug the network and you still have 33 searchable documents.
SOURCE HEALTH says 1 of 4 feeds live. What is wrong with the other three?
I checked each from inside the container. The two RBI feeds return HTTP 418 — RBI's bot protection rejects any non-browser client, so those two are structurally dead for a server-side fetcher and would need a headless browser or a paid data source. The PIB all-India feed returns HTTP 200 with a valid but EMPTY RSS channel — that is upstream, PIB simply publishes nothing on Regid=0. PIB Gujarat returns 20 items reliably, which happens to be the regionally relevant one. The panel shows 1/4 rather than hiding it, which is the honest thing for a feed-health tile to do.
What is the difference between the "linked", "same family" and "similar" badges on related documents?
Three different mechanisms, deliberately labelled so you know which one fired. "linked" at 100% is a curated hand-authored relationship in the corpus — the I4C e-Zero FIR notification is explicitly linked to its Gujarat implementation and to the 1930 helpline. "same family" means the documents share a statutory lineage — IT Act 2000 to the e-Zero FIR notification. "similar" with a percentage is pure vector similarity from the embedding store. So a judge can tell curated ground truth from machine inference at a glance, rather than us presenting both as "related".
Search returns 24 results for everything. Type gibberish and it still returns 24.
Correct, and that is the sharpest flaw on this screen. Retrieval returns exactly k results with no relevance floor — "zzzzqqq nothing" comes back with 24 documents at 15-16%. The score badge on each card is the honest signal and it collapses to noise, but the count line does not. The fix is a similarity cutoff in the search service, roughly a five-line change, and it is not in yet. What the ranking does get right is order: real queries put the right document first at 55-62% every time.
Why does "Central" say 24 documents when the tile says Central 27?
The search request is capped at k=24 for page performance, so 27 central documents get truncated to the first 24 and the count line reports what was returned rather than what matched. Same for Gujarat's 26. There is no pagination on this panel — you narrow with a query or a filter instead. The insights tile is the source of truth for corpus totals.
You clicked Refresh and the "last fetch" timestamp did not move.
Because that tile is MAX(fetched_at) across documents — it is the time the newest document entered the index, not the time we last polled. The refresh genuinely ran (it reported "Fetched 20, 0 new") and found nothing new, so no document got a new fetched_at. Mislabelled tile, not a broken refresh. The per-feed last_attempt timestamps in the API did update.
Is the AI summary generated by a model?
Not by an LLM — llm_online is false. It is extractive: it selects and condenses sentences from the stored document body and renders them as bullets, in 15 ms, offline. If a Gemini key were configured the badge would flip to "Gemini summaries" and the same button would produce an abstractive summary in the selected language. Today the language selector changes only the button label; the text stays English. I would rather tell you that than click it and hope.
Who can refresh the feeds? Could a citizen trigger 20 outbound requests?
No. POST /api/gov/refresh is gated require_role("lead") server-side, and the front end matches that rank so the button is not even rendered for an officer — I verified both by logging in as officer@city.gov. Search, bookmarks, subscriptions and saved searches are available to any signed-in user, which is deliberate: this is the one module a citizen account can also reach.
Those trending chips — are they real or hardcoded?
Real. They are the top eight queries by frequency from the search log, with a seed list backfilling when the log is thin. That is honest but it also means anything typed during a demo becomes a chip — my own test queries polluted it, including a gibberish string, and I flushed them by re-running the curated terms. Worth knowing before someone types something silly on stage.
How fast is search?
58 ms median for a 24-result semantic search over 53 documents, measured server-side across 5 runs. Related-document lookup is 62 ms and the summary is 15 ms. It is ChromaDB on local disk — no network, no API bill.

Careful / known limits

  • THE DEFAULT LANDING VIEW IS THE WORST THING ON THIS SCREEN. With no query, the top of the results grid is 20 live PIB Gujarat press releases in Hindi about the BRICS Youth Summit, an air-quality commission ruling and a Hindi advisory committee meeting — none of them policing-related, and one of them duplicated. The presenter MUST type a query before the judges read the screen.
  • One duplicated live document: the same PIB release "गुजरात के गांधीनगर में ब्रिक्स युवा परिषद की बैठक…" appears twice, because PIB publishes it under two PRIDs and the dedupe key hashes the link.
  • No relevance floor. "zzzzqqq nothing" returns 24 documents at 15-16%. The count line reads "24 document(s)" for literally any input.
  • k=24 cap with no pagination. Region "Central" shows 24 of 27 and "Gujarat" 24 of 26; 29 of the 53 documents are unreachable from the default view without a query or filter.
  • Doc-type chip counts on first load reflect the current 24-document window (Act ·5, Judgment ·2) not the corpus (Act 8, Judgment 4). Clicking the chip then shows the right number, so the label contradicts itself for one click.
  • 3 of 4 RSS feeds are dead. Both RBI feeds return HTTP 418 (bot block) from inside the container — permanently, not transiently. PIB all-India returns a valid but empty channel upstream. Only PIB Gujarat delivers. The tile honestly says "1/4 feeds live" but a judge will ask.
  • The language selector has no effect on the AI summary — hi and gu produce byte-identical English. Only the button label changes. Do not click it.
  • "last fetch" in the SOURCE HEALTH tile is MAX(fetched_at) over documents, not the last poll time. Clicking Refresh with 0 new documents leaves it unchanged, which reads as "the refresh did nothing".
  • Alerts cannot be demonstrated live. Refresh reports "0 alert(s) sent" because it finds 0 new documents; alerts only fire on genuinely new matching content. Show the saved search with its "alerting" pill instead and explain the trigger.
  • RATE LIMIT WARNING (environmental, not a code bug in this module): nginx does not forward the real client IP to FastAPI, so every browser session shares ONE 120 req/min bucket. With other agents testing concurrently the panel showed "Too many requests" in the results area and the insights strip went blank. Close every other browser tab and stop any background agents before the demo — a single presenter will never hit it. To its credit, the panel surfaces the error rather than silently blanking (unlike CrimeGPT).
  • Note on demo state I left behind, deliberately: case #29's CrimeGPT pool is populated, Legal Feed has 2 bookmarks, 1 saved search with alerting enabled and 1 subscription on the admin account, and the trending chips have been flushed to curated terms. All of that makes the demo look better, not worse — but it is my data, not seed data.
  • Nothing crashed. Zero JavaScript pageerrors across every Legal Feed interaction tested.

Measured numbers you can quote

  • Bundled corpus: 33 documents (verified up from 25) — Act 8, Scheme 8, Notification 7, Judgment 4, Circular 2, GR 2, Rule 2
  • Source links: 33/33 verified reachable — 32 returned HTTP 200 to a script, all 10 spot-checked in a real browser returned 200 with the correct page title
  • Total indexed after live pull: 53 documents (33 bundled + 20 live), Central 27 / Gujarat 26
  • Live RSS: 20 items from PIB Gujarat; 1 of 4 feeds delivers content (2× RBI blocked with HTTP 418, PIB all-India returns an empty channel)
  • Search latency: 58 ms median server-side for k=24 (5 runs; min 56, max 80)
  • Related-documents latency: 62 ms median (5 runs); AI summary 15 ms median
  • Refresh feeds: 1.4 s end-to-end, idempotent ("Fetched 20, 0 new, 0 alert(s) sent" on re-run)
  • Department dropdown: 21 real issuing authorities + "All departments"
  • Top relevance scores measured: 62% (Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita, DPDP Act), 61% (1930 helpline), 57% (RBI digital lending), 56% (I4C e-Zero FIR)

Cases

The case file: 10 cases (4 active, 6 closed) with per-case evidence, an officer↔citizen message thread, a staff-only audit timeline, verdict on close, and per-row citizen visibility enforced server-side.

Get there: Sign in as admin@city.gov / admin123 → top nav "Cases". Case list → click a case card → tabs Evidence / Documents / Messages / Schedule / Timeline.

Show this

  1. Open closed case #33 and walk the four tabs — 4 evidence items (2 CCTV keyframes + the source clip + a note), a 3-message thread across officer→citizen→lead, and a timeline signed by the officer who did it.
    Do: Cases → click "#33 · Violent altercation reported in dense locality" → Evidence → Messages → Timeline.
    It is a complete chain of custody, not a mock-up: the evidence rows point at real ingested clips (cctv_overhead_01.mp4) and every action is attributed to a named user with a timestamp.
  2. The citizen-visibility toggle is row-level, not a hidden button. Case #31 holds 5 evidence rows; the citizen logged in at the same moment sees exactly 1 — the one tagged visibility=citizen.
    Do: As admin: case #31 → Evidence (5 rows). Second browser as citizen@example.com / citizen123 → My Cases → same case → Evidence (1 row: "Seizure panchnama drawn…").
    Filtering happens in the API (svc.evidence_visible_to), so a judge who opens devtools sees the server return one row, not four rows with three CSS-hidden.
  3. Close a case live: click "Close case", type a verdict, confirm — status flips to Closed, the verdict renders in a green block in the header, and a close_case row appears in the timeline attributed to you, all in ~2s.
    Do: Open an active case (e.g. #29) → Close case → paste verdict text → Confirm close.
    The verdict is the legally meaningful field and it is written into the audit log verbatim; the demo shows write→audit→render in one click.
  4. The staff view and the citizen view of the SAME case are different objects: staff get 5 tabs (Evidence, Documents, Messages, Schedule, Timeline), citizens get 2 (Evidence, Messages) and a star-rating box; officers get everything except "Close case".
    Three role behaviours on one screen, and every one of them is also refused by the API (measured 403s — see the RBAC page).
  5. Meeting scheduler inside the case (Schedule tab) that notifies every assigned case member.
    Small, but it is the piece that turns a case record into actual station workflow. Note it is EMPTY on every seeded case — only show it if you book one live.
